debug_assert_matches
 # The Rust Standard Library
I The Rust Standard Library is the foundation of portable Rust software, a
K set of minimal and battle-tested shared abstractions for the [broader Rust
A ecosystem][crates.io]. It offers core types, like [`Vec<T>`] and
7 [`Option<T>`], library-defined [operations on language
@ primitives](#primitives), [standard macros](#macros), [I/O] and
4 [multithreading], among [many other things][other].
A `std` is available to all Rust crates by default. Therefore, the
H standard library can be accessed in [`use`] statements through the path
 `std`, as in [`use std::env`].
! # How to read this documentation
M If you already know the name of what you are looking for, the fastest way to
O find it is to use the <a href="#" onclick="window.searchState.focus();">search
# button</a> at the top of the page.
A Otherwise, you may want to jump to one of these useful sections:
 * [`std::*` modules](#modules)
! * [Primitive types](#primitives)
 * [Standard macros](#macros)
 * [The Rust Prelude]
J If this is your first time, the documentation for the standard library is
F written to be casually perused. Clicking on interesting things should
J generally lead you to interesting places. Still, there are important bits
J you don't want to miss, so read on for a tour of the standard library and
 its documentation!
H Once you are familiar with the contents of the standard library you may
L begin to find the verbosity of the prose distracting. At this stage in your
& development you may want to press the
 "<svg style="width:0.75rem;height:0.75rem" viewBox="0 0 12 12" stroke="currentColor" fill="none"><path d="M2,2l4,4l4,-4M2,6l4,4l4,-4"/></svg>&nbsp;Summary"
K button near the top of the page to collapse it into a more skimmable view.
> While you are looking at the top of the page, also notice the
> "Source" link. Rust's API documentation comes with the source
G code and you are encouraged to read it. The standard library source is
9 generally high quality and a peek behind the curtains is
 often enlightening.
1 # What is in the standard library documentation?
L First of all, The Rust Standard Library is divided into a number of focused
J modules, [all listed further down this page](#modules). These modules are
I the bedrock upon which all of Rust is forged, and they have mighty names
G like [`std::slice`] and [`std::cmp`]. Modules' documentation typically
H includes an overview of the module along with examples, and are a smart
8 place to start familiarizing yourself with the library.
L Second, implicit methods on [primitive types] are documented here. This can
* be a source of confusion for two reasons:
J 1. While primitives are implemented by the compiler, the standard library
J    implements methods directly on the primitive types (and it is the only
B    library that does so), which are [documented in the section on
    primitives](#primitives).
D 2. The standard library exports many modules *with the same name as
L    primitive types*. These define additional items related to the primitive
,    type, but not the all-important methods.
7 So for example there is a [page for the primitive type
J `char`](primitive::char) that lists all the methods that can be called on
> characters (very useful), and there is a [page for the module
[ `std::char`](crate::char) that documents iterator and error types created by these methods
 (rarely useful).
P Note the documentation for the primitives [`str`] and [`[T]`][prim@slice] (also
M called 'slice'). Many method calls on [`String`] and [`Vec<T>`] are actually
M calls to methods on [`str`] and [`[T]`][prim@slice] respectively, via [deref
 coercions][deref-coercions].
K Third, the standard library defines [The Rust Prelude], a small collection
H of items - mostly traits - that are imported into every module of every
C crate. The traits in the prelude are pervasive, making the prelude
@ documentation a good entry point to learning about the library.
K And finally, the standard library exports a number of standard macros, and
I [lists them on this page](#macros) (technically, not all of the standard
E macros are defined by the standard library - some are defined by the
I compiler - but they are documented here the same). Like the prelude, the
9 standard macros are imported by default into all crates.
, # Contributing changes to the documentation
3 Check out the Rust contribution guidelines [here](
P https://rustc-dev-guide.rust-lang.org/contributing.html#writing-documentation).
2 The source for this documentation can be found on
M [GitHub](https://github.com/rust-lang/rust) in the 'library/std/' directory.
L To contribute changes, make sure you read the guidelines first, then submit
* pull-requests for your suggested changes.
I Contributions are appreciated! If you see a part of the docs that can be
F improved, submit a PR, or chat with us first on [Zulip][t-libs-zulip]
	 #t-libs.
& # A Tour of The Rust Standard Library
J The rest of this crate documentation is dedicated to pointing out notable
' features of The Rust Standard Library.
 ## Containers and collections
I The [`option`] and [`result`] modules define optional and error-handling
G types, [`Option<T>`] and [`Result<T, E>`]. The [`iter`] module defines
J Rust's iterator trait, [`Iterator`], which works with the [`for`] loop to
 access collections.
G The standard library exposes three common ways to deal with contiguous
 regions of memory:
G * [`Vec<T>`] - A heap-allocated *vector* that is resizable at runtime.
P * [`[T; N]`][prim@array] - An inline *array* with a fixed size at compile time.
V * [`[T]`][prim@slice] - A dynamically sized *slice* into any other kind of contiguous
*   storage, whether heap-allocated or not.
L Slices can only be handled through some kind of *pointer*, and as such come
 in many flavors such as:
 * `&[T]` - *shared slice*
 * `&mut [T]` - *mutable slice*
, * [`Box<[T]>`][owned slice] - *owned slice*
M [`str`], a UTF-8 string slice, is a primitive type, and the standard library
E defines many methods for it. Rust [`str`]s are typically accessed as
H immutable references: `&str`. Use the owned [`String`] for building and
 mutating strings.
M For converting to strings use the [`format!`] macro, and for converting from
# strings use the [`FromStr`] trait.
J Data may be shared by placing it in a reference-counted box or the [`Rc`]
L type, and if further contained in a [`Cell`] or [`RefCell`], may be mutated
M as well as shared. Likewise, in a concurrent setting it is common to pair an
L atomically-reference-counted box, [`Arc`], with a [`Mutex`] to get the same
 effect.
F The [`collections`] module defines maps, sets, linked lists and other
B typical collection types, including the common [`HashMap<K, V>`].
! ## Platform abstractions and I/O
I Besides basic data types, the standard library is largely concerned with
K abstracting over differences in common platforms, most notably Windows and
 Unix derivatives.
I Common types of I/O, including [files], [TCP], and [UDP], are defined in
) the [`io`], [`fs`], and [`net`] modules.
G The [`thread`] module contains Rust's threading abstractions. [`sync`]
S contains further primitive shared memory types, including [`atomic`], [`mpmc`] and
@ [`mpsc`], which contains the channel types for message passing.
  # Use before and after `main()`
S Many parts of the standard library are expected to work before and after `main()`;
` but this is not guaranteed or ensured by tests. It is recommended that you write your own tests
3 and run them on each platform you wish to support.
^ This means that use of `std` before/after main, especially of features that interact with the
[ OS or global state, is exempted from stability and portability guarantees and instead only
K provided on a best-effort basis. Nevertheless bug reports are appreciated.
W On the other hand `core` and `alloc` are most likely to work in such environments with
[ the caveat that any hookable behavior such as panics, oom handling or allocators will also
* depend on the compatibility of the hooks.
\ Some features may also behave differently outside main, e.g. stdio could become unbuffered,
V some panics might turn into aborts, backtraces might not get symbolicated or similar.
* Non-exhaustive list of known limitations:
K - after-main use of thread-locals, which also affects additional features:
   - [`thread::current()`]
I - under UNIX, before main, file descriptors 0, 1, and 2 may be unchanged
/   (they are guaranteed to be open during main,
M    and are opened to /dev/null O_RDWR if they weren't open on program start)

 # The Rust Prelude
I Rust comes with a variety of things in its standard library. However, if
I you had to manually import every single thing that you used, it would be
L very verbose. But importing a lot of things that a program never uses isn't
+ good either. A balance needs to be struck.
I The *prelude* is the list of things that Rust automatically imports into
F every Rust program. It's kept as small as possible, and is focused on
H things, particularly traits, which are used in almost every single Rust
	 program.
 # Other preludes
D Preludes can be seen as a pattern to make using multiple types more
I convenient. As such, you'll find other preludes in the standard library,
J such as [`std::io::prelude`]. Various libraries in the Rust ecosystem may
  also define their own preludes.
) [`std::io::prelude`]: crate::io::prelude
K The difference between 'the prelude' and these other preludes is that they
L are not automatically `use`'d, and must be imported manually. This is still
; easier than importing all of their constituent components.
 # Prelude contents
F The items included in the prelude depend on the edition of the crate.
E The first version of the prelude is used in Rust 2015 and Rust 2018,
# and lives in [`std::prelude::v1`].
T [`std::prelude::rust_2015`] and [`std::prelude::rust_2018`] re-export this prelude.
 It re-exports the following:
J * <code>[std::marker]::{[Copy], [Send], [Sized], [Sync], [Unpin]}</code>,
?   marker traits that indicate fundamental properties of types.
J * <code>[std::ops]::{[Fn], [FnMut], [FnOnce]}</code>, and their analogous
S   async traits, <code>[std::ops]::{[AsyncFn], [AsyncFnMut], [AsyncFnOnce]}</code>.
A * <code>[std::ops]::[Drop]</code>, for implementing destructors.
I * <code>[std::mem]::[drop]</code>, a convenience function for explicitly
   dropping a value.
J * <code>[std::mem]::{[size_of], [size_of_val]}</code>, to get the size of
   a type or value.
D * <code>[std::mem]::{[align_of], [align_of_val]}</code>, to get the
    alignment of a type or value.
J * <code>[std::boxed]::[Box]</code>, a way to allocate values on the heap.
K * <code>[std::borrow]::[ToOwned]</code>, the conversion trait that defines
E   [`to_owned`], the generic method for creating an owned type from a
   borrowed type.
H * <code>[std::clone]::[Clone]</code>, the ubiquitous trait that defines
G   [`clone`][Clone::clone], the method for producing a copy of a value.
I * <code>[std::cmp]::{[PartialEq], [PartialOrd], [Eq], [Ord]}</code>, the
L   comparison traits, which implement the comparison operators and are often
   seen in trait bounds.
K * <code>[std::convert]::{[AsRef], [AsMut], [Into], [From]}</code>, generic
G   conversions, used by savvy API authors to create overloaded methods.
J * <code>[std::default]::[Default]</code>, types that have default values.
S * <code>[std::iter]::{[Iterator], [Extend], [IntoIterator], [DoubleEndedIterator],
;   [ExactSizeIterator]}</code>, iterators of various kinds.
 * Most of the standard macros.
L * <code>[std::option]::[Option]::{[self][Option], [Some], [None]}</code>, a
K   type which expresses the presence or absence of a value. This type is so
1   commonly used, its variants are also exported.
N * <code>[std::result]::[Result]::{[self][Result], [Ok], [Err]}</code>, a type
L   for functions that may succeed or fail. Like [`Option`], its variants are
   exported as well.
N * <code>[std::string]::{[String], [ToString]}</code>, heap-allocated strings.
E * <code>[std::vec]::[Vec]</code>, a growable, heap-allocated vector.
W The prelude used in Rust 2021, [`std::prelude::rust_2021`], includes all of the above,
 and in addition re-exports:
7 * <code>[std::convert]::{[TryFrom], [TryInto]}</code>.
, * <code>[std::iter]::[FromIterator]</code>.
W The prelude used in Rust 2024, [`std::prelude::rust_2024`], includes all of the above,
8 * <code>[std::future]::{[Future], [IntoFuture]}</code>.

!Panics the current thread.
This allows a program to terminate immediately and provide feedback
to the caller of the program.
This macro is the perfect way to assert conditions in example code and in
tests. `panic!` is closely tied with the `unwrap` method of both
[`Option`][ounwrap] and [`Result`][runwrap] enums. Both implementations call
`panic!` when they are set to [`None`] or [`Err`] variants.
When using `panic!()` you can specify a string payload that is built using
[formatting syntax]. That payload is used when injecting the panic into
the calling Rust thread, causing the thread to panic entirely.
The behavior of the default `std` hook, i.e. the code that runs directly
after the panic is invoked, is to print the message payload to
`stderr` along with the file/line/column information of the `panic!()`
call. You can override the panic hook using [`std::panic::set_hook()`].
Inside the hook a panic can be accessed as a `&dyn Any + Send`,
which contains either a `&str` or `String` for regular `panic!()` invocations.
(Whether a particular invocation contains the payload at type `&str` or `String` is unspecified and can change.)
To panic with a value of another other type, [`panic_any`] can be used.
See also the macro [`compile_error!`], for raising errors during compilation.
# When to use `panic!` vs `Result`
The Rust language provides two complementary systems for constructing /
representing, reporting, propagating, reacting to, and discarding errors. These
responsibilities are collectively known as "error handling." `panic!` and
`Result` are similar in that they are each the primary interface of their
respective error handling systems; however, the meaning these interfaces attach
to their errors and the responsibilities they fulfill within their respective
error handling systems differ.
The `panic!` macro is used to construct errors that represent a bug that has
been detected in your program. With `panic!` you provide a message that
describes the bug and the language then constructs an error with that message,
reports it, and propagates it for you.
`Result` on the other hand is used to wrap other types that represent either
the successful result of some computation, `Ok(T)`, or error types that
represent an anticipated runtime failure mode of that computation, `Err(E)`.
`Result` is used alongside user defined types which represent the various
anticipated runtime failure modes that the associated computation could
encounter. `Result` must be propagated manually, often with the help of the
`?` operator and `Try` trait, and they must be reported manually, often with
the help of the `Error` trait.
For more detailed information about error handling check out the [book] or the
[`std::result`] module docs.
[ounwrap]: Option::unwrap
[runwrap]: Result::unwrap
[`std::panic::set_hook()`]: ../std/panic/fn.set_hook.html
[`panic_any`]: ../std/panic/fn.panic_any.html
[`Box`]: ../std/boxed/struct.Box.html
[`Any`]: crate::any::Any
[formatting syntax]: ../std/fmt/index.html
[book]: ../book/ch09-00-error-handling.html
[`std::result`]: ../std/result/index.html
# Current implementation
If the main thread panics it will terminate all your threads and end your
program with code `101`.
# Editions
Behavior of the panic macros changed over editions.
## 2021 and later
In Rust 2021 and later, `panic!` always requires a format string and
the applicable format arguments, and is the same in `core` and `std`.
Use [`std::panic::panic_any(x)`](../std/panic/fn.panic_any.html) to
panic with an arbitrary payload.
## 2018 and 2015
In Rust Editions prior to 2021, `std::panic!(x)` with a single
argument directly uses that argument as a payload.
This is true even if the argument is a string literal.
For example, `panic!("problem: {reason}")` panics with a
payload of literally `"problem: {reason}"` (a `&'static str`).
`core::panic!(x)` with a single argument requires that `x` be `&str`,
but otherwise behaves like `std::panic!`. In particular, the string
need not be a literal, and is not interpreted as a format string.

F Compute the distance between the origin and a point (`x`, `y`) on the
I Euclidean plane. Equivalently, compute the length of the hypotenuse of a

C;_T
E Calculates the least nonnegative remainder of `self` when divided by
 `rhs`.
H In particular, the return value `r` satisfies `0.0 <= r < rhs.abs()` in
D most cases. However, due to a floating point round-off error it can
F result in `r == rhs.abs()`, violating the mathematical definition, if
G `self` is much smaller than `rhs.abs()` in magnitude and `self < 0.0`.
H This result is not an element of the function's codomain, but it is the
H closest floating point number in the real numbers and thus fulfills the
E property `self == self.div_euclid(rhs) * rhs + self.rem_euclid(rhs)`
 approximately.

 Native threads.
 ## The threading model
I An executing Rust program consists of a collection of native OS threads,
E each with their own stack and local state. Threads can be named, and
= provide some built-in support for low-level synchronization.
2 Communication between threads can be done through
L [channels], Rust's message-passing types, along with [other forms of thread
C synchronization](../../std/sync/index.html) and shared-memory data
; structures. In particular, types that are guaranteed to be
7 threadsafe are easily shared between threads using the
1 atomically-reference-counted container, [`Arc`].
> Fatal logic errors in Rust cause *thread panic*, during which
@ a thread will unwind the stack, running destructors and freeing
D owned resources. While not meant as a 'try/catch' mechanism, panics
M in Rust can nonetheless be caught (unless compiling with `panic=abort`) with
E [`catch_unwind`](../../std/panic/fn.catch_unwind.html) and recovered
' from, or alternatively be resumed with
G [`resume_unwind`](../../std/panic/fn.resume_unwind.html). If the panic
D is not caught the thread will exit, but the panic may optionally be
J detected from a different thread with [`join`]. If the main thread panics
A without the panic being caught, the application will exit with a
 non-zero exit code.
L When the main thread of a Rust program terminates, the entire program shuts
M down, even if other threads are still running. However, this module provides
I convenient facilities for automatically waiting for the termination of a
 thread (i.e., join).
 ## Spawning a thread
K A new thread can be spawned using the [`thread::spawn`][`spawn`] function:
 use std::thread;
 thread::spawn(move || {
     // some work here
 });
M In this example, the spawned thread is "detached," which means that there is
O no way for the program to learn when the spawned thread completes or otherwise
 terminates.
P To learn when a thread completes, it is necessary to capture the [`JoinHandle`]
A object that is returned by the call to [`spawn`], which provides
I a `join` method that allows the caller to wait for the completion of the
 spawned thread:
1 let thread_join_handle = thread::spawn(move || {
 // some work here
% let res = thread_join_handle.join();
P The [`join`] method returns a [`thread::Result`] containing [`Ok`] of the final
G value produced by the spawned thread, or [`Err`] of the value given to
- a call to [`panic!`] if the thread panicked.
O Note that there is no parent/child relationship between a thread that spawns a
S new thread and the thread being spawned.  In particular, the spawned thread may or
T may not outlive the spawning thread, unless the spawning thread is the main thread.
 ## Configuring threads
N A new thread can be configured before it is spawned via the [`Builder`] type,
J which currently allows you to set the name and stack size for the thread:
 # #![allow(unused_must_use)]
C thread::Builder::new().name("thread1".to_string()).spawn(move || {
     println!("Hello, world!");
 ## The `Thread` type
M Threads are represented via the [`Thread`] type, which you can get in one of
 two ways:
G * By spawning a new thread, e.g., using the [`thread::spawn`][`spawn`]
P   function, and calling [`thread`][`JoinHandle::thread`] on the [`JoinHandle`].
L * By requesting the current thread, using the [`thread::current`] function.
K The [`thread::current`] function is available even for threads not spawned
 by the APIs of this module.
 ## Thread-local storage
M This module also provides an implementation of thread-local storage for Rust
I programs. Thread-local storage is a method of storing data into a global
D variable that each thread in the program will have its own copy of.
L Threads do not share this data, so accesses do not need to be synchronized.
R A thread-local key owns the value it contains and will destroy the value when the
Q thread exits. It is created with the [`thread_local!`] macro and can contain any
R value that is `'static` (no borrowed pointers). It provides an accessor function,
G [`with`], that yields a shared reference to the value to the specified
T closure. Thread-local keys allow only shared access to values, as there would be no
I way to guarantee uniqueness if mutable borrows were allowed. Most values
J will want to make use of some form of **interior mutability** through the
 [`Cell`] or [`RefCell`] types.
 ## Naming threads
[ Threads are able to have associated names for identification purposes. By default, spawned
` threads are unnamed. To specify a name for a thread, build the thread with [`Builder`] and pass
Z the desired thread name to [`Builder::name`]. To retrieve the thread name from within the
Y thread, use [`Thread::name`]. A couple of examples where the name of a thread gets used:
] * If a panic occurs in a named thread, the thread name will be printed in the panic message.
X * The thread name is provided to the OS where applicable (e.g., `pthread_setname_np` in
   unix-like platforms).
 ## Stack size
D The default stack size is platform-dependent and subject to change.
0 Currently, it is 2 MiB on all Tier-1 platforms.
K There are two ways to manually specify the stack size for spawned threads:
` * Build the thread with [`Builder`] and pass the desired stack size to [`Builder::stack_size`].
] * Set the `RUST_MIN_STACK` environment variable to an integer representing the desired stack
_   size (in bytes). Note that setting [`Builder::stack_size`] will override this. Be aware that
B   changes to `RUST_MIN_STACK` may be ignored after program start.
I Note that the stack size of the main thread is *not* determined by Rust.

! # Initialization and Destruction
L Initialization is dynamically performed on the first call to a setter (e.g.
B [`with`]) within a thread, and values that implement [`Drop`] get
L destructed when a thread exits. Some platform-specific caveats apply, which
 are explained below.
M Note that, should the destructor panic, the whole process will be [aborted].
F On platforms where initialization requires memory allocation, this is
G performed directly through [`System`], allowing the [global allocator]
% to make use of thread local storage.
H A `LocalKey`'s initializer cannot recursively depend on itself. Using a
J `LocalKey` in this way may cause panics, aborts, or infinite recursion on
 the first call to `with`.
! [`System`]: crate::alloc::System

! [aborted]: crate::process::abort
  # Single-thread Synchronization
N Though there is no potential race with other threads, it is still possible to
K obtain multiple references to the thread-local data in different places on
P the call stack. For this reason, only shared (`&T`) references may be obtained.
J To allow obtaining an exclusive mutable reference (`&mut T`), typically a
L [`Cell`] or [`RefCell`] is used (see the [`std::cell`] for more information
F on how exactly this works). To make this easier there are specialized
H implementations for [`LocalKey<Cell<T>>`] and [`LocalKey<RefCell<T>>`].

 # Platform-specific behavior
G Note that a "best effort" is made to ensure that destructors for types
L stored in thread local storage are run, but not all platforms can guarantee
H that destructors will be run for all types in thread local storage. For
L example, there are a number of known caveats where destructors are not run:
J 1. On Unix systems when pthread-based TLS is being used, destructors will
M    not be run for TLS values on the main thread when it exits. Note that the
J    application will exit immediately after the main thread exits as well.
K 2. On all platforms it's possible for TLS to re-initialize other TLS slots
E    during destruction. Some platforms ensure that this cannot happen
H    infinitely by preventing re-initialization of any slot that has been
M    destroyed, but not all platforms have this guard. Those platforms that do
H    not guard typically have a synthetic limit after which point no more
    destructors are run.
J 3. When the process exits on Windows systems, TLS destructors may only be
J    run on the thread that causes the process to exit. This is because the
-    other threads may be forcibly terminated.
/ ## Synchronization in thread-local destructors
I On Windows, synchronization operations (such as [`JoinHandle::join`]) in
J thread local destructors are prone to deadlocks and so should be avoided.
I This is because the [loader lock] is held while a destructor is run. The
K lock is acquired whenever a thread starts or exits or when a DLL is loaded
H or unloaded. Therefore these events are blocked for as long as a thread
 local destructor is running.
g [loader lock]: https://docs.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/dlls/dynamic-link-library-best-practices

8 Spawns a new thread, returning a [`JoinHandle`] for it.
P The join handle provides a [`join`] method that can be used to join the spawned
Q thread. If the spawned thread panics, [`join`] will return an [`Err`] containing
" the argument given to [`panic!`].
Q If the join handle is dropped, the spawned thread will implicitly be *detached*.
: In this case, the spawned thread may no longer be joined.
N (It is the responsibility of the program to either eventually join threads it
A creates or detach them; otherwise, a resource leak will result.)
K This function creates a thread with the default parameters of [`Builder`].
L To specify the new thread's stack size or the name, use [`Builder::spawn`].
H As you can see in the signature of `spawn` there are two constraints on
L both the closure given to `spawn` and its return value, let's explain them:
G - The `'static` constraint means that the closure and its return value
K   must have a lifetime of the whole program execution. The reason for this
F   is that threads can outlive the lifetime they have been created in.
M   Indeed if the thread, and by extension its return value, can outlive their
M   caller, we need to make sure that they will be valid afterwards, and since
L   we *can't* know when it will return we need to have them valid as long as
F   possible, that is until the end of the program, hence the `'static`
   lifetime.
H - The [`Send`] constraint is because the closure will need to be passed
H   *by value* from the thread where it is spawned to the new thread. Its
H   return value will need to be passed from the new thread to the thread
   where it is `join`ed.
K   As a reminder, the [`Send`] marker trait expresses that it is safe to be
M   passed from thread to thread. [`Sync`] expresses that it is safe to have a
-   reference be passed from thread to thread.
B Panics if the OS fails to create a thread; use [`Builder::spawn`]
 to recover from such errors.

[ different runtime) as [`catch_unwind`]; namely, if the thread created with `thread::spawn`
[ unwinds all the way to the root with such an exception, one of two behaviors are possible,

8 Cooperatively gives up a timeslice to the OS scheduler.
D This calls the underlying OS scheduler's yield primitive, signaling
F that the calling thread is willing to give up its remaining timeslice
6 so that the OS may schedule other threads on the CPU.
E A drawback of yielding in a loop is that if the OS does not have any
K other ready threads to run on the current CPU, the thread will effectively
- busy-wait, which wastes CPU time and energy.
E Therefore, when waiting for events of interest, a programmer's first
F choice should be to use synchronization devices such as [`channel`]s,
A [`Condvar`]s, [`Mutex`]es or [`join`] since these primitives are
D implemented in a blocking manner, giving up the CPU until the event
9 of interest has occurred which avoids repeated yielding.
H `yield_now` should thus be used only rarely, mostly in situations where
G repeated polling is required because there is no other suitable way to
. learn when an event of interest has occurred.
 thread::yield_now();

" [`Condvar`]: crate::sync::Condvar
 [`Mutex`]: crate::sync::Mutex

E Blocks unless or until the current thread's token is made available.
G A call to `park` does not guarantee that the thread will remain parked
G forever, and callers should be prepared for this possibility. However,
E it is guaranteed that this function will not panic (it may abort the
< process if the implementation encounters some rare errors).
 # `park` and `unpark`
M Every thread is equipped with some basic low-level blocking support, via the
K [`thread::park`][`park`] function and [`thread::Thread::unpark`][`unpark`]
K method. [`park`] blocks the current thread, which can then be resumed from
H another thread by calling the [`unpark`] method on the blocked thread's
 handle.
G Conceptually, each [`Thread`] handle has an associated token, which is
 initially not present:
L * The [`thread::park`][`park`] function blocks the current thread unless or
H   until the token is available for its thread handle, at which point it
J   atomically consumes the token. It may also return *spuriously*, without
J   consuming the token. [`thread::park_timeout`] does the same, but allows
5   specifying a maximum time to block the thread for.
M * The [`unpark`] method on a [`Thread`] atomically makes the token available
^   if it wasn't already. Because the token can be held by a thread even if it is currently not
`   parked, [`unpark`] followed by [`park`] will result in the second call returning immediately.
a   However, note that to rely on this guarantee, you need to make sure that your `unpark` happens
>   after all `park` that may be done by other data structures!
` The API is typically used by acquiring a handle to the current thread, placing that handle in a
a shared data structure so that other threads can find it, and then `park`ing in a loop. When some
_ desired condition is met, another thread calls [`unpark`] on the handle. The last bullet point
^ above guarantees that even if the `unpark` occurs before the thread is finished `park`ing, it
 will be woken up properly.
^ Note that the coordination via the shared data structure is crucial: If you `unpark` a thread
` without first establishing that it is about to be `park`ing within your code, that `unpark` may
` get consumed by a *different* `park` in the same thread, leading to a deadlock. This also means
` you must not call unknown code between setting up for parking and calling `park`; for instance,
a if you invoke `println!`, that may itself call `park` and thus consume your `unpark` and cause a
 deadlock.
+ The motivation for this design is twofold:
H * It avoids the need to allocate mutexes and condvars when building new
@   synchronization primitives; the threads already provide basic
   blocking/signaling.
< * It can be implemented very efficiently on many platforms.
 # Memory Ordering
J Calls to `unpark` _synchronize-with_ calls to `park`, meaning that memory
S operations performed before a call to `unpark` are made visible to the thread that
N consumes the token and returns from `park`. Note that all `park` and `unpark`
U operations for a given thread form a total order and _all_ prior `unpark` operations
 synchronize-with `park`.
M In atomic ordering terms, `unpark` performs a `Release` operation and `park`
O performs the corresponding `Acquire` operation. Calls to `unpark` for the same
" thread form a [release sequence].
N Note that being unblocked does not imply a call was made to `unpark`, because
E wakeups can also be spurious. For example, a valid, but inefficient,
Y implementation could have `park` and `unpark` return immediately without doing anything,
 making *all* wakeups spurious.
/ use std::sync::atomic::{Ordering, AtomicBool};

4 static QUEUED: AtomicBool = AtomicBool::new(false);
2 static FLAG: AtomicBool = AtomicBool::new(false);

+     QUEUED.store(true, Ordering::Release);
N     // We want to wait until the flag is set. We *could* just spin, but using
&     // park/unpark is more efficient.
*     while !FLAG.load(Ordering::Acquire) {
\         // We can *not* use `println!` here since that could use thread parking internally.
         thread::park();
U         // We *could* get here spuriously, i.e., way before the 10ms below are over!
R         // But that is no problem, we are in a loop until the flag is set anyway.
     println!("Flag received");

* thread::sleep(Duration::from_millis(10));
' // Ensure the thread is about to park.
J // This is crucial! It guarantees that the `unpark` below is not consumed
E // by some other code in the parked thread (e.g. inside `println!`).

Q     // Spinning is of course inefficient; in practice, this would more likely be
G     // a dequeue where we have no work to do if there's nobody queued.
     std::hint::spin_loop();

O Returns an estimate of the default amount of parallelism a program should use.
K Parallelism is a resource. A given machine provides a certain capacity for
H parallelism, i.e., a bound on the number of computations it can perform
F simultaneously. This number often corresponds to the amount of CPUs a
3 computer has, but it may diverge in various cases.
E Host environments such as VMs or container orchestrators may want to
L restrict the amount of parallelism made available to programs in them. This
A is often done to limit the potential impact of (unintentionally)
K resource-intensive programs on other programs running on the same machine.
 # Limitations
H The purpose of this API is to provide an easy and portable way to query
P the default amount of parallelism the program should use. Among other things it
B does not expose information on NUMA regions, does not account for
B differences in (co)processor capabilities or current system load,
K and will not modify the program's global state in order to more accurately
+ query the amount of available parallelism.
N Where both fixed steady-state and burst limits are available the steady-state
< capacity will be used to ensure more predictable latencies.
W Resource limits can be changed during the runtime of a program, therefore the value is
W not cached and instead recomputed every time this function is called. It should not be
 called from hot code.
F The value returned by this function should be considered a simplified
I approximation of the actual amount of parallelism available at any given
B time. To get a more detailed or precise overview of the amount of
: parallelism available to the program, you may wish to use
M platform-specific APIs as well. The following platform limitations currently
" apply to `available_parallelism`:
 On Windows:
M - It may undercount the amount of parallelism available on systems with more
M   than 64 logical CPUs. However, programs typically need specific support to
J   take advantage of more than 64 logical CPUs, and in the absence of such
H   support, the number returned by this function accurately reflects the
9   number of logical CPUs the program can use by default.
M - It may overcount the amount of parallelism available on systems limited by
:   process-wide affinity masks, or job object limitations.
 On Linux:
I - It may overcount the amount of parallelism available when limited by a
^   process-wide affinity mask or cgroup quotas and `sched_getaffinity()` or cgroup fs can't be
#   queried, e.g. due to sandboxing.
T - It may undercount the amount of parallelism if the current thread's affinity mask
D   does not reflect the process' cpuset, e.g. due to pinned threads.
D - If the process is in a cgroup v1 cpu controller, this may need to
C   scan mountpoints to find the corresponding cgroup v1 controller,
D   which may take time on systems with large numbers of mountpoints.
>   (This does not apply to cgroup v2, or to processes not in a
   cgroup.)
^ - It does not attempt to take `ulimit` into account. If there is a limit set on the number of
\   threads, `available_parallelism` cannot know how much of that limit a Rust program should
Y   take, or know in a reliable and race-free way how much of that limit is already taken.
 On all targets:
L - It may overcount the amount of parallelism available when running in a VM
4 with CPU usage limits (e.g. an overcommitted host).
J This function will, but is not limited to, return errors in the following
 cases:
E - If the amount of parallelism is not known for the target platform.
J - If the program lacks permission to query the amount of parallelism made
   available to it.
 # #![allow(dead_code)]
 use std::{io, thread};
 fn main() -> io::Result<()> {
8     let count = thread::available_parallelism()?.get();
     assert!(count >= 1_usize);
     Ok(())

 # Lifetimes
; Scoped threads involve two lifetimes: `'scope` and `'env`.
C The `'scope` lifetime represents the lifetime of the scope itself.
B That is: the time during which new scoped threads may be spawned,
< and also the time during which they might still be running.
8 Once this lifetime ends, all scoped threads are joined.
_ This lifetime starts within the `scope` function, before `f` (the argument to `scope`) starts.
_ It ends after `f` returns and all scoped threads have been joined, but before `scope` returns.
[ The `'env` lifetime represents the lifetime of whatever is borrowed by the scoped threads.
Z This lifetime must outlast the call to `scope`, and thus cannot be smaller than `'scope`.
Z It can be as small as the call to `scope`, meaning that anything that outlives this call,
_ such as local variables defined right before the scope, can be borrowed by the scoped threads.
H The `'env: 'scope` bound is part of the definition of the `Scope` type.

8 Support for capturing a stack backtrace of an OS thread
M This module contains the support necessary to capture a stack backtrace of a
K running OS thread from the OS thread itself. The `Backtrace` type supports
9 capturing a stack trace via the `Backtrace::capture` and
& `Backtrace::force_capture` functions.
E A backtrace is typically quite handy to attach to errors (e.g. types
J implementing `std::error::Error`) to get a causal chain of where an error
 was generated.
 ## Accuracy
J Backtraces are attempted to be as accurate as possible, but no guarantees
L are provided about the exact accuracy of a backtrace. Instruction pointers,
F symbol names, filenames, line numbers, etc, may all be incorrect when
I reported. Accuracy is attempted on a best-effort basis, however, any bug
= reports are always welcome to indicate areas of improvement!
I For most platforms a backtrace with a filename/line number requires that
G programs be compiled with debug information. Without debug information
- filenames/line numbers will not be reported.
 ## Platform support
K Not all platforms that std compiles for support capturing backtraces. Some
M platforms simply do nothing when capturing a backtrace. To check whether the
M platform supports capturing backtraces you can consult the `BacktraceStatus`
) enum as a result of `Backtrace::status`.
J Like above with accuracy platform support is done on a best effort basis.
J Sometimes libraries might not be available at runtime or something may go
L wrong which would cause a backtrace to not be captured. Please feel free to
J report issues with platforms where a backtrace cannot be captured though!
 ## Environment Variables
L The `Backtrace::capture` function might not actually capture a backtrace by
@ default. Its behavior is governed by two environment variables:
I * `RUST_LIB_BACKTRACE` - if this is set to `0` then `Backtrace::capture`
B   will never capture a backtrace. Any other value set will enable
   `Backtrace::capture`.
L * `RUST_BACKTRACE` - if `RUST_LIB_BACKTRACE` is not set, then this variable
<   is consulted with the same rules of `RUST_LIB_BACKTRACE`.
K * If neither of the above env vars are set, then `Backtrace::capture` will
   be disabled.
I Capturing a backtrace can be a quite expensive runtime operation, so the
C environment variables allow either forcibly disabling this runtime
C performance hit or allow selectively enabling it in some programs.
H Note that the `Backtrace::force_capture` function can be used to ignore
E these environment variables. Also note that the state of environment
E variables is cached once the first backtrace is created, so altering
N `RUST_LIB_BACKTRACE` or `RUST_BACKTRACE` at runtime might not actually change
 how backtraces are captured.

 Collection types.
M Rust's standard collection library provides efficient implementations of the
F most common general purpose programming data structures. By using the
E standard implementations, it should be possible for two libraries to
1 communicate without significant data conversion.
Q To get this out of the way: you should probably just use [`Vec`] or [`HashMap`].
H These two collections cover most use cases for generic data storage and
M processing. They are exceptionally good at doing what they do. All the other
K collections in the standard library have specific use cases where they are
J the optimal choice, but these cases are borderline *niche* in comparison.
M Even when `Vec` and `HashMap` are technically suboptimal, they're probably a
# good enough choice to get started.
> Rust's collections can be grouped into four major categories:
3 * Sequences: [`Vec`], [`VecDeque`], [`LinkedList`]
" * Maps: [`HashMap`], [`BTreeMap`]
" * Sets: [`HashSet`], [`BTreeSet`]
 * Misc: [`BinaryHeap`]
( # When Should You Use Which Collection?
J These are fairly high-level and quick break-downs of when each collection
J should be considered. Detailed discussions of strengths and weaknesses of
F individual collections can be found on their own documentation pages.
 ### Use a [`Vec`] when:
L * You want to collect items up to be processed or sent elsewhere later, and
E   don't care about any properties of the actual values being stored.
J * You want a sequence of elements in a particular order, and will only be
"   appending to (or near) the end.
 * You want a stack.
 * You want a resizable array.
# * You want a heap-allocated array.
 ### Use a [`VecDeque`] when:
K * You want a [`Vec`] that supports efficient insertion at both ends of the
   sequence.
 * You want a queue.
) * You want a double-ended queue (deque).
 ### Use a [`LinkedList`] when:
I * You want a [`Vec`] or [`VecDeque`] of unknown size, and can't tolerate
   amortization.
2 * You want to efficiently split and append lists.
K * You are *absolutely* certain you *really*, *truly*, want a doubly linked
   list.
 ### Use a [`HashMap`] when:
@ * You want to associate arbitrary keys with an arbitrary value.
 * You want a cache.
/ * You want a map, with no extra functionality.
 ### Use a [`BTreeMap`] when:
% * You want a map sorted by its keys.
; * You want to be able to get a range of entries on-demand.
G * You're interested in what the smallest or largest key-value pair is.
I * You want to find the largest or smallest key that is smaller or larger
   than something.
7 ### Use the `Set` variant of any of these `Map`s when:
4 * You just want to remember which keys you've seen.
< * There is no meaningful value to associate with your keys.
 * You just want a set.
 ### Use a [`BinaryHeap`] when:
K * You want to store a bunch of elements, but only ever want to process the
7   "biggest" or "most important" one at any given time.
 * You want a priority queue.
 # Performance
L Choosing the right collection for the job requires an understanding of what
I each collection is good at. Here we briefly summarize the performance of
M different collections for certain important operations. For further details,
M see each type's documentation, and note that the names of actual methods may
5 differ from the tables below on certain collections.
J Throughout the documentation, we will adhere to the following conventions
 for operation notation:
+ * The collection's size is denoted by `n`.
B * If a second collection is involved, its size is denoted by `m`.
# * Item indices are denoted by `i`.
E * Operations which have an *amortized* cost are suffixed with a `*`.
> * Operations with an *expected* cost are suffixed with a `~`.
H Calling operations that add to a collection will occasionally require a
H collection to be resized - an extra operation that takes *O*(*n*) time.
M *Amortized* costs are calculated to account for the time cost of such resize
K operations *over a sufficiently large series of operations*. An individual
K operation may be slower or faster due to the sporadic nature of collection
M resizing, however the average cost per operation will approach the amortized
 cost.
L Rust's collections never automatically shrink, so removal operations aren't
 amortized.
M [`HashMap`] uses *expected* costs. It is theoretically possible, though very
M unlikely, for [`HashMap`] to experience significantly worse performance than
M the expected cost. This is due to the probabilistic nature of hashing - i.e.
K it is possible to generate a duplicate hash given some input key that will
& require extra computation to correct.
! ## Cost of Collection Operations
 |                | get(i)                 | insert(i)               | remove(i)              | append(Vec(m))    | split_off(i)           | range           | append       |
 |----------------|------------------------|-------------------------|------------------------|-------------------|------------------------|-----------------|--------------|
 | [`Vec`]        | *O*(1)                 | *O*(*n*-*i*)*           | *O*(*n*-*i*)           | *O*(*m*)*         | *O*(*n*-*i*)           | N/A             | N/A          |
 | [`VecDeque`]   | *O*(1)                 | *O*(min(*i*, *n*-*i*))* | *O*(min(*i*, *n*-*i*)) | *O*(*m*)*         | *O*(min(*i*, *n*-*i*)) | N/A             | N/A          |
 | [`LinkedList`] | *O*(min(*i*, *n*-*i*)) | *O*(min(*i*, *n*-*i*))  | *O*(min(*i*, *n*-*i*)) | *O*(1)            | *O*(min(*i*, *n*-*i*)) | N/A             | N/A          |
 | [`HashMap`]    | *O*(1)~                | *O*(1)~*                | *O*(1)~                | N/A               | N/A                    | N/A             | N/A          |
 | [`BTreeMap`]   | *O*(log(*n*))          | *O*(log(*n*))           | *O*(log(*n*))          | N/A               | N/A                    | *O*(log(*n*))   | *O*(*n*+*m*) |
I Note that where ties occur, [`Vec`] is generally going to be faster than
D [`VecDeque`], and [`VecDeque`] is generally going to be faster than
 [`LinkedList`].
H For Sets, all operations have the cost of the equivalent Map operation.
- # Correct and Efficient Usage of Collections
I Of course, knowing which collection is the right one for the job doesn't
G instantly permit you to use it correctly. Here are some quick tips for
G efficient and correct usage of the standard collections in general. If
M you're interested in how to use a specific collection in particular, consult
= its documentation for detailed discussion and code examples.
 ## Capacity Management
H Many collections provide several constructors and methods that refer to
F "capacity". These collections are generally built on top of an array.
F Optimally, this array would be exactly the right size to fit only the
K elements stored in the collection, but for the collection to do this would
L be very inefficient. If the backing array was exactly the right size at all
L times, then every time an element is inserted, the collection would have to
L grow the array to fit it. Due to the way memory is allocated and managed on
L most computers, this would almost surely require allocating an entirely new
J array and copying every single element from the old one into the new one.
J Hopefully you can see that this wouldn't be very efficient to do on every
H Most collections therefore use an *amortized* allocation strategy. They
M generally let themselves have a fair amount of unoccupied space so that they
B only have to grow on occasion. When they do grow, they allocate a
L substantially larger array to move the elements into so that it will take a
G while for another grow to be required. While this strategy is great in
M general, it would be even better if the collection *never* had to resize its
H backing array. Unfortunately, the collection itself doesn't have enough
M information to do this itself. Therefore, it is up to us programmers to give
 it hints.
I Any `with_capacity` constructor will instruct the collection to allocate
L enough space for the specified number of elements. Ideally this will be for
H exactly that many elements, but some implementation details may prevent
I this. See collection-specific documentation for details. In general, use
M `with_capacity` when you know exactly how many elements will be inserted, or
7 at least have a reasonable upper-bound on that number.
F When anticipating a large influx of elements, the `reserve` family of
K methods can be used to hint to the collection how much room it should make
G for the coming items. As with `with_capacity`, the precise behavior of
> these methods will be specific to the collection of interest.
D For optimal performance, collections will generally avoid shrinking
L themselves. If you believe that a collection will not soon contain any more
M elements, or just really need the memory, the `shrink_to_fit` method prompts
J the collection to shrink the backing array to the minimum size capable of
 holding its elements.
F Finally, if ever you're interested in what the actual capacity of the
J collection is, most collections provide a `capacity` method to query this
I information on demand. This can be useful for debugging purposes, or for
  use with the `reserve` methods.
 ## Iterators
 [Iterators][crate::iter]
; are a powerful and robust mechanism used throughout Rust's
I standard libraries. Iterators provide a sequence of values in a generic,
L safe, efficient and convenient way. The contents of an iterator are usually
I *lazily* evaluated, so that only the values that are actually needed are
L ever actually produced, and no allocation need be done to temporarily store
I them. Iterators are primarily consumed using a `for` loop, although many
J functions also take iterators where a collection or sequence of values is
	 desired.
I All of the standard collections provide several iterators for performing
H bulk manipulation of their contents. The three primary iterators almost
I every collection should provide are `iter`, `iter_mut`, and `into_iter`.
K Some of these are not provided on collections where it would be unsound or
 unreasonable to provide them.
M `iter` provides an iterator of immutable references to all the contents of a
O collection in the most "natural" order. For sequence collections like [`Vec`],
K this means the items will be yielded in increasing order of index starting
K at 0. For ordered collections like [`BTreeMap`], this means that the items
M will be yielded in sorted order. For unordered collections like [`HashMap`],
M the items will be yielded in whatever order the internal representation made
K most convenient. This is great for reading through all the contents of the
 collection.
 let vec = vec![1, 2, 3, 4];
 for x in vec.iter() {
$    println!("vec contained {x:?}");
M `iter_mut` provides an iterator of *mutable* references in the same order as
G `iter`. This is great for mutating all the contents of the collection.
  let mut vec = vec![1, 2, 3, 4];
 for x in vec.iter_mut() {
    *x += 1;
G `into_iter` transforms the actual collection into an iterator over its
I contents by-value. This is great when the collection itself is no longer
M needed, and the values are needed elsewhere. Using `extend` with `into_iter`
H is the main way that contents of one collection are moved into another.
X `extend` automatically calls `into_iter`, and takes any <code>T: [IntoIterator]</code>.
K Calling `collect` on an iterator itself is also a great way to convert one
I collection into another. Both of these methods should internally use the
J capacity management tools discussed in the previous section to do this as
 efficiently as possible.
! let mut vec1 = vec![1, 2, 3, 4];
! let vec2 = vec![10, 20, 30, 40];
 vec1.extend(vec2);
  use std::collections::VecDeque;
 let vec = [1, 2, 3, 4];
2 let buf: VecDeque<_> = vec.into_iter().collect();
K Iterators also provide a series of *adapter* methods for performing common
N threads to sequences. Among the adapters are functional favorites like `map`,
H `fold`, `skip` and `take`. Of particular interest to collections is the
N `rev` adapter, which reverses any iterator that supports this operation. Most
L collections provide reversible iterators as the way to iterate over them in
 reverse order.
 for x in vec.iter().rev() {
K Several other collection methods also return iterators to yield a sequence
M of results but avoid allocating an entire collection to store the result in.
% This provides maximum flexibility as
/ [`collect`][crate::iter::Iterator::collect] or
9 [`extend`][crate::iter::Extend::extend] can be called to
L "pipe" the sequence into any collection if desired. Otherwise, the sequence
I can be looped over with a `for` loop. The iterator can also be discarded
C after partial use, preventing the computation of the unused items.
 ## Entries
B The `entry` API is intended to provide an efficient mechanism for
M manipulating the contents of a map conditionally on the presence of a key or
F not. The primary motivating use case for this is to provide efficient
I accumulator maps. For instance, if one wishes to maintain a count of the
G number of times each key has been seen, they will have to perform some
M conditional logic on whether this is the first time the key has been seen or
D not. Normally, this would require a `find` followed by an `insert`,
= effectively duplicating the search effort on each insertion.
H When a user calls `map.entry(key)`, the map will search for the key and
* then yield a variant of the `Entry` enum.
L If a `Vacant(entry)` is yielded, then the key *was not* found. In this case
M the only valid operation is to `insert` a value into the entry. When this is
M done, the vacant entry is consumed and converted into a mutable reference to
I the value that was inserted. This allows for further manipulation of the
J value beyond the lifetime of the search itself. This is useful if complex
M logic needs to be performed on the value regardless of whether the value was
 just inserted.
L If an `Occupied(entry)` is yielded, then the key *was* found. In this case,
G the user has several options: they can `get`, `insert` or `remove` the
I value of the occupied entry. Additionally, they can convert the occupied
G entry into a mutable reference to its value, providing symmetry to the
 vacant `insert` case.
 ### Examples
H Here are the two primary ways in which `entry` is used. First, a simple
< example where the logic performed on the values is trivial.
D #### Counting the number of times each character in a string occurs
+ use std::collections::btree_map::BTreeMap;
! let mut count = BTreeMap::new();
7 let message = "she sells sea shells by the sea shore";
 for c in message.chars() {
'     *count.entry(c).or_insert(0) += 1;
' assert_eq!(count.get(&'s'), Some(&8));
5 println!("Number of occurrences of each character");
 for (char, count) in &count {
!     println!("{char}: {count}");
K When the logic to be performed on the value is more complex, we may simply
L use the `entry` API to ensure that the value is initialized and perform the
 logic afterwards.

Index
A A [hash map] implemented with quadratic probing and SIMD lookup.
C By default, `HashMap` uses a hashing algorithm selected to provide
L resistance against HashDoS attacks. The algorithm is randomly seeded, and a
J reasonable best-effort is made to generate this seed from a high quality,
F secure source of randomness provided by the host without blocking the
K program. Because of this, the randomness of the seed depends on the output
J quality of the system's random number generator when the seed is created.
L In particular, seeds generated when the system's entropy pool is abnormally
: low such as during system boot may be of a lower quality.
G The default hashing algorithm is currently SipHash 1-3, though this is
L subject to change at any point in the future. While its performance is very
L competitive for medium sized keys, other hashing algorithms will outperform
F it for small keys such as integers as well as large keys such as long
F strings, though those algorithms will typically *not* protect against
 attacks such as HashDoS.
I The hashing algorithm can be replaced on a per-`HashMap` basis using the
H [`default`], [`with_hasher`], and [`with_capacity_and_hasher`] methods.
H There are many alternative [hashing algorithms available on crates.io].
P It is required that the keys implement the [`Eq`] and [`Hash`] traits, although
K this can frequently be achieved by using `#[derive(PartialEq, Eq, Hash)]`.
D If you implement these yourself, it is important that the following
 property holds:
 ```text
! k1 == k2 -> hash(k1) == hash(k2)
C In other words, if two keys are equal, their hashes must be equal.
* Violating this property is a logic error.
O It is also a logic error for a key to be modified in such a way that the key's
M hash, as determined by the [`Hash`] trait, or its equality, as determined by
H the [`Eq`] trait, changes while it is in the map. This is normally only
K possible through [`Cell`], [`RefCell`], global state, I/O, or unsafe code.
J The behavior resulting from either logic error is not specified, but will
G be encapsulated to the `HashMap` that observed the logic error and not
L result in undefined behavior. This could include panics, incorrect results,
+ aborts, memory leaks, and non-termination.

= This function is sound to call in a single-threaded program.
J This function is also always sound to call on Windows, in single-threaded
 and multi-threaded programs.
P In multi-threaded programs on other operating systems, the only sound option is
- to not use `set_var` or `remove_var` at all.
 The exact requirement is: you
D must ensure that there are no other threads concurrently writing or
I *reading*(!) the environment through functions or global variables other
J than the ones in this module. The problem is that these operating systems
E do not provide a thread-safe way to read the environment, and most C
H libraries, including libc itself, do not advertise which functions read
H from the environment. Even functions from the Rust standard library may
E read the environment without going through this module, e.g. for DNS
L lookups from [`std::net::ToSocketAddrs`]. No stable guarantee is made about
F which functions may read from the environment in future versions of a
I library. All this makes it not practically possible for you to guarantee
L that no other thread will read the environment, so the only sound option is
H to not use `set_var` or `remove_var` in multi-threaded programs at all.

.Interfaces for working with Errors.
# Error Handling In Rust
The Rust language provides two complementary systems for constructing /
representing, reporting, propagating, reacting to, and discarding errors.
These responsibilities are collectively known as "error handling." The
components of the first system, the panic runtime and interfaces, are most
commonly used to represent bugs that have been detected in your program. The
components of the second system, `Result`, the error traits, and user
defined types, are used to represent anticipated runtime failure modes of
your program.
## The Panic Interfaces
The following are the primary interfaces of the panic system and the
responsibilities they cover:
* [`panic!`] and [`panic_any`] (Constructing, Propagated automatically)
* [`set_hook`], [`take_hook`], and [`PanicHookInfo`] (Reporting)
* [`#[panic_handler]`][panic-handler] and [`PanicInfo`] (Reporting in no_std)
* [`catch_unwind`] and [`resume_unwind`] (Discarding, Propagating)
The following are the primary interfaces of the error system and the
responsibilities they cover:
* [`Result`] (Propagating, Reacting)
* The [`Error`] trait (Reporting)
* User defined types (Constructing / Representing)
* [`match`] and [`downcast`] (Reacting)
* The question mark operator ([`?`]) (Propagating)
* The partially stable [`Try`] traits (Propagating, Constructing)
* [`Termination`] (Reporting)
## Converting Errors into Panics
The panic and error systems are not entirely distinct. Often times errors
that are anticipated runtime failures in an API might instead represent bugs
to a caller. For these situations the standard library provides APIs for
constructing panics with an `Error` as its source.
* [`Result::unwrap`]
* [`Result::expect`]
These functions are equivalent, they either return the inner value if the
`Result` is `Ok` or panic if the `Result` is `Err` printing the inner error
as the source. The only difference between them is that with `expect` you
provide a panic error message to be printed alongside the source, whereas
`unwrap` has a default message indicating only that you unwrapped an `Err`.
Of the two, `expect` is generally preferred since its `msg` field allows you
to convey your intent and assumptions which makes tracking down the source
of a panic easier. `unwrap` on the other hand can still be a good fit in
situations where you can trivially show that a piece of code will never
panic, such as `"127.0.0.1".parse::<std::net::IpAddr>().unwrap()` or early
prototyping.
# Common Message Styles
There are two common styles for how people word `expect` messages. Using
the message to present information to users encountering a panic
("expect as error message") or using the message to present information
to developers debugging the panic ("expect as precondition").
In the former case the expect message is used to describe the error that
has occurred which is considered a bug. Consider the following example:
```should_panic
// Read environment variable, panic if it is not present
let path = std::env::var("IMPORTANT_PATH").unwrap();
In the "expect as error message" style we would use expect to describe
that the environment variable was not set when it should have been:
```should_panic
let path = std::env::var("IMPORTANT_PATH")
    .expect("env variable `IMPORTANT_PATH` is not set");
In the "expect as precondition" style, we would instead describe the
reason we _expect_ the `Result` should be `Ok`. With this style we would
prefer to write:
```should_panic
let path = std::env::var("IMPORTANT_PATH")
    .expect("env variable `IMPORTANT_PATH` should be set by `wrapper_script.sh`");
The "expect as error message" style does not work as well with the
default output of the std panic hooks, and often ends up repeating
information that is already communicated by the source error being
unwrapped:
```text
thread 'main' panicked at src/main.rs:4:6:
env variable `IMPORTANT_PATH` is not set: NotPresent
In this example we end up mentioning that an env variable is not set,
followed by our source message that says the env is not present, the
only additional information we're communicating is the name of the
environment variable being checked.
The "expect as precondition" style instead focuses on source code
readability, making it easier to understand what must have gone wrong in
situations where panics are being used to represent bugs exclusively.
Also, by framing our expect in terms of what "SHOULD" have happened to
prevent the source error, we end up introducing new information that is
independent from our source error.
```text
thread 'main' panicked at src/main.rs:4:6:
env variable `IMPORTANT_PATH` should be set by `wrapper_script.sh`: NotPresent
In this example we are communicating not only the name of the
environment variable that should have been set, but also an explanation
for why it should have been set, and we let the source error display as
a clear contradiction to our expectation.
**Hint**: If you're having trouble remembering how to phrase
expect-as-precondition style error messages remember to focus on the word
"should" as in "env variable should be set by blah" or "the given binary
should be available and executable by the current user".

# Utilities related to FFI bindings.
> This module provides utilities to handle data across non-Rust
@ interfaces, like other programming languages and the underlying
@ operating system. It is mainly of use for FFI (Foreign Function
C Interface) bindings and code that needs to exchange C-like strings
 with other languages.
 # Overview
< Rust represents owned strings with the [`String`] type, and
@ borrowed slices of strings with the [`str`] primitive. Both are
C always in UTF-8 encoding, and may contain nul bytes in the middle,
B i.e., if you look at the bytes that make up the string, there may
A be a `\0` among them. Both `String` and `str` store their length
? explicitly; there are no nul terminators at the end of strings
 like in C.
+ C strings are different from Rust strings:
@ * **Encodings** - Rust strings are UTF-8, but C strings may use
> other encodings. If you are using a string from C, you should
A check its encoding explicitly, rather than just assuming that it
" is UTF-8 like you can do in Rust.
C * **Character size** - C strings may use `char` or `wchar_t`-sized
F characters; please **note** that C's `char` is different from Rust's.
> The C standard leaves the actual sizes of those types open to
B interpretation, but defines different APIs for strings made up of
A each character type. Rust strings are always UTF-8, so different
A Unicode characters will be encoded in a variable number of bytes
; each. The Rust type [`char`] represents a '[Unicode scalar
? value]', which is similar to, but not the same as, a '[Unicode
 code point]'.
= * **Nul terminators and implicit string lengths** - Often, C
D strings are nul-terminated, i.e., they have a `\0` character at the
@ end. The length of a string buffer is not stored, but has to be
; calculated; to compute the length of a string, C code must
C manually call a function like `strlen()` for `char`-based strings,
? or `wcslen()` for `wchar_t`-based ones. Those functions return
9 the number of characters in the string excluding the nul
? terminator, so the buffer length is really `len+1` characters.
A Rust strings don't have a nul terminator; their length is always
9 stored and does not need to be calculated. While in Rust
@ accessing a string's length is an *O*(1) operation (because the
@ length is stored); in C it is an *O*(*n*) operation because the
? length needs to be computed by scanning the string for the nul
 terminator.
: * **Internal nul characters** - When C strings have a nul
C terminator character, this usually means that they cannot have nul
? characters in the middle 
 a nul character would essentially
? truncate the string. Rust strings *can* have nul characters in
= the middle, because nul does not have to mark the end of the
 string in Rust.
& # Representations of non-Rust strings
> [`CString`] and [`CStr`] are useful when you need to transfer
? UTF-8 strings to and from languages with a C ABI, like Python.
B * **From Rust to C:** [`CString`] represents an owned, C-friendly
B string: it is nul-terminated, and has no internal nul characters.
D Rust code can create a [`CString`] out of a normal string (provided
@ that the string doesn't have nul characters in the middle), and
O then use a variety of methods to obtain a raw <code>\*mut [u8]</code> that can
; then be passed as an argument to functions which use the C
 conventions for strings.
B * **From C to Rust:** [`CStr`] represents a borrowed C string; it
P is what you would use to wrap a raw <code>\*const [u8]</code> that you got from
D a C function. A [`CStr`] is guaranteed to be a nul-terminated array
A of bytes. Once you have a [`CStr`], you can convert it to a Rust
I <code>&[str]</code> if it's valid UTF-8, or lossily convert it by adding
 replacement characters.
@ [`OsString`] and [`OsStr`] are useful when you need to transfer
C strings to and from the operating system itself, or when capturing
C the output of external commands. Conversions between [`OsString`],
C [`OsStr`] and Rust strings work similarly to those for [`CString`]
 and [`CStr`].
M * [`OsString`] losslessly represents an owned platform string. However, this
D representation is not necessarily in a form native to the platform.
W In the Rust standard library, various APIs that transfer strings to/from the operating
? system use [`OsString`] instead of plain strings. For example,
= [`env::var_os()`] is used to query environment variables; it
J returns an <code>[Option]<[OsString]></code>. If the environment variable
E exists you will get a <code>[Some]\(os_string)</code>, which you can
J *then* try to convert to a Rust string. This yields a [`Result`], so that
A your code can detect errors in case the environment variable did
( not in fact contain valid Unicode data.
M * [`OsStr`] losslessly represents a borrowed reference to a platform string.
R However, this representation is not necessarily in a form native to the platform.
G It can be converted into a UTF-8 Rust string slice in a similar way to
 [`OsString`].
 # Conversions
 ## On Unix
" On Unix, [`OsStr`] implements the
H <code>std::os::unix::ffi::[OsStrExt][unix.OsStrExt]</code> trait, which
? augments it with two methods, [`from_bytes`] and [`as_bytes`].
: These do inexpensive conversions from and to byte slices.
2 Additionally, on Unix [`OsString`] implements the
H <code>std::os::unix::ffi::[OsStringExt][unix.OsStringExt]</code> trait,
B which provides [`from_vec`] and [`into_vec`] methods that consume
8 their arguments, and take or produce vectors of [`u8`].
 ## On Windows
I An [`OsStr`] can be losslessly converted to a native Windows string. And
H a native Windows string can be losslessly converted to an [`OsString`].
% On Windows, [`OsStr`] implements the
H <code>std::os::windows::ffi::[OsStrExt][windows.OsStrExt]</code> trait,
; which provides an [`encode_wide`] method. This provides an
I iterator that can be [`collect`]ed into a vector of [`u16`]. After a nul
E characters is appended, this is the same as a native Windows string.
5 Additionally, on Windows [`OsString`] implements the
F <code>std::os::windows:ffi::[OsStringExt][windows.OsStringExt]</code>
I trait, which provides a [`from_wide`] method to convert a native Windows
C string (without the terminating nul character) to an [`OsString`].
 ## Other platforms
= Many other platforms provide their own extension traits in a
 `std::os::*::ffi` module.
 ## On all platforms
] On all platforms, [`OsStr`] consists of a sequence of bytes that is encoded as a superset of
Q UTF-8; see [`OsString`] for more details on its encoding on different platforms.
\ For limited, inexpensive conversions from and to bytes, see [`OsStr::as_encoded_bytes`] and
) [`OsStr::from_encoded_bytes_unchecked`].
A For basic string processing, see [`OsStr::slice_encoded_bytes`].

FromStr
I A type that can represent owned, mutable platform-native strings, but is
- cheaply inter-convertible with Rust strings.
2 The need for this type arises from the fact that:
E * On Unix systems, strings are often arbitrary sequences of non-zero
-   bytes, in many cases interpreted as UTF-8.
G * On Windows, strings are often arbitrary sequences of non-zero 16-bit
;   values, interpreted as UTF-16 when it is valid to do so.
D * In Rust, strings are always valid UTF-8, which may contain zeros.
M `OsString` and [`OsStr`] bridge this gap by simultaneously representing Rust
L and platform-native string values, and in particular allowing a Rust string
L to be converted into an "OS" string with no cost if possible. A consequence
J of this is that `OsString` instances are *not* `NUL` terminated; in order
A to pass to e.g., Unix system call, you should create a [`CStr`].
[ `OsString` is to <code>&[OsStr]</code> as [`String`] is to <code>&[str]</code>: the former
8 in each pair are owned strings; the latter are borrowed
 references.
M Note, `OsString` and [`OsStr`] internally do not necessarily hold strings in
H the form native to the platform; While on Unix, strings are stored as a
K sequence of 8-bit values, on Windows, where strings are 16-bit value based
K as just discussed, strings are also actually stored as a sequence of 8-bit
E values, encoded in a less-strict variant of UTF-8. This is useful to
5 understand when handling capacity and length values.
 # Capacity of `OsString`
] Capacity uses units of UTF-8 bytes for OS strings which were created from valid unicode, and
Z uses units of bytes in an unspecified encoding for other contents. On a given target, all
[ `OsString` and `OsStr` values use the same units for capacity, so the following will work:
! use std::ffi::{OsStr, OsString};

3     ret.push(a); // This will not allocate further
3     ret.push(b); // This will not allocate further
     ret

$ Filesystem manipulation operations.

( # Time of Check to Time of Use (TOCTOU)
c Many filesystem operations are subject to a race condition known as "Time of Check to Time of Use"
] (TOCTOU). This occurs when a program checks a condition (like file existence or permissions)
^ and then uses the result of that check to make a decision, but the condition may have changed
 between the check and the use.
[ For example, checking if a file exists and then creating it if it doesn't is vulnerable to
X TOCTOU - another process could create the file between your check and creation attempt.
_ Another example is with symbolic links: when removing a directory, if another process replaces
\ the directory with a symbolic link between the check and the removal operation, the removal
\ might affect the wrong location. This is why operations like [`remove_dir_all`] need to use
3 atomic operations to prevent such race conditions.
 To avoid TOCTOU issues:
c - Be aware that metadata operations (like [`metadata`] or [`symlink_metadata`]) may be affected by
! changes made by other processes.
o - Use atomic operations when possible (like [`File::create_new`] instead of checking existence then creating).
1 - Keep file open for the duration of operations.

% Sets the option for the append mode.
H This option, when true, means that writes will append to a file instead
" of overwriting previous contents.
E Note that setting `.write(true).append(true)` has the same effect as
 setting only `.append(true)`.
R Append mode guarantees that writes will be positioned at the current end of file,
S even when there are other processes or threads appending to the same file. This is
P unlike <code>[seek]\([SeekFrom]::[End]\(0))</code> followed by `write()`, which
S has a race between seeking and writing during which another writer can write, with
& our `write()` overwriting their data.
L Keep in mind that this does not necessarily guarantee that data appended by
R different processes or threads does not interleave. The amount of data accepted a
I single `write()` call depends on the operating system and file system. A
Q successful `write()` is allowed to write only part of the given data, so even if
Q you're careful to provide the whole message in a single call to `write()`, there
S is no guarantee that it will be written out in full. If you rely on the filesystem
N accepting the message in a single write, make sure that all data that belongs
P together is written in one operation. This can be done by concatenating strings
$ before passing them to [`write()`].
H If a file is opened with both read and append access, beware that after
K opening, and after every write, the position for reading may be set at the
F end of the file. So, before writing, save the current position (using
N <code>[Seek]::[stream_position]</code>), and restore it before the next read.
 ## Note
C This function doesn't create the file if it doesn't exist. Use the
) [`OpenOptions::create`] method to do so.

- [`flush()`]: Write::flush "io::Write::flush"

 ## Symlinks
D On UNIX-like systems, this function will update the permission bits
' of the file pointed to by the symlink.
J Note that this behavior can lead to privilege escalation vulnerabilities,
E where the ability to create a symlink in one directory allows you to
C cause the permissions of another file or directory to be modified.
F For this reason, using this function with symlinks should be avoided.
C When possible, permissions should be set at creation time instead.

I and symlinks can be followed regardless of what permission bits are set.
