B Traits, helpers, and type definitions for core I/O functionality.
D The `std::io` module contains a number of common things you'll need
B when doing input and output. The most core part of this module is
5 the [`Read`] and [`Write`] traits, which provide the
A most general interface for reading and writing input and output.
 ## Read and Write
L Because they are traits, [`Read`] and [`Write`] are implemented by a number
H of other types, and you can implement them for your types too. As such,
H you'll see a few different types of I/O throughout the documentation in
L this module: [`File`]s, [`TcpStream`]s, and sometimes even [`Vec<T>`]s. For
L example, [`Read`] adds a [`read`][`Read::read`] method, which we can use on
 [`File`]s:
     let mut buffer = [0; 10];
     // read up to 10 bytes
"     let n = f.read(&mut buffer)?;
/     println!("The bytes: {:?}", &buffer[..n]);
O [`Read`] and [`Write`] are so important, implementors of the two traits have a
J nickname: readers and writers. So you'll sometimes see 'a reader' instead
= of 'a type that implements the [`Read`] trait'. Much easier!
 ## Seek and BufRead
H Beyond that, there are two important traits that are provided: [`Seek`]
C and [`BufRead`]. Both of these build on top of a reader to control
J how the reading happens. [`Seek`] lets you control where the next byte is
 coming from:
-     // skip to the last 10 bytes of the file
!     f.seek(SeekFrom::End(-10))?;
S [`BufRead`] uses an internal buffer to provide a number of other ways to read, but
K to show it off, we'll need to talk about buffers in general. Keep reading!
 ## BufReader and BufWriter
N Byte-based interfaces are unwieldy and can be inefficient, as we'd need to be
G making near-constant calls to the operating system. To help with this,
N `std::io` comes with two structs, [`BufReader`] and [`BufWriter`], which wrap
G readers and writers. The wrapper uses a buffer, reducing the number of
G calls and providing nicer methods for accessing exactly what you want.
I For example, [`BufReader`] works with the [`BufRead`] trait to add extra
 methods to any reader:
 use std::io::BufReader;
(     let mut reader = BufReader::new(f);
$     let mut buffer = String::new();
     // read a line into buffer
$     reader.read_line(&mut buffer)?;
     println!("{buffer}");
N [`BufWriter`] doesn't add any new ways of writing; it just buffers every call
 to [`write`][`Write::write`]:
 use std::io::BufWriter;
,         let mut writer = BufWriter::new(f);
&         // write a byte to the buffer
         writer.write(&[42])?;
=     } // the buffer is flushed once writer goes out of scope
 ## Standard input and output
1 A very common source of input is standard input:
#     let mut input = String::new();
(     io::stdin().read_line(&mut input)?;
-     println!("You typed: {}", input.trim());
L Note that you cannot use the [`?` operator] in functions that do not return
B a [`Result<T, E>`][`Result`]. Instead, you can call [`.unwrap()`]
= or `match` on the return value to catch any possible errors:
 let mut input = String::new();
, io::stdin().read_line(&mut input).unwrap();
7 And a very common source of output is standard output:
      io::stdout().write(&[42])?;
L Of course, using [`io::stdout`] directly is less common than something like
 [`println!`].
 ## Iterator types
G A large number of the structures provided by `std::io` are for various
I ways of iterating over I/O. For example, [`Lines`] is used to split over
 lines:
$     let reader = BufReader::new(f);
!     for line in reader.lines() {
         println!("{}", line?);
 ## Functions
O There are a number of [functions][functions-list] that offer access to various
N features. For example, we can use three of these functions to copy everything
( from standard input to standard output:
4     io::copy(&mut io::stdin(), &mut io::stdout())?;
 [functions-list]: #functions-1
 ## io::Result
D Last, but certainly not least, is [`io::Result`]. This type is used
L as the return type of many `std::io` functions that can cause an error, and
N can be returned from your own functions as well. Many of the examples in this
 module use the [`?` operator]:
$ fn read_input() -> io::Result<()> {
O The return type of `read_input()`, [`io::Result<()>`][`io::Result`], is a very
Q common type for functions which don't have a 'real' return value, but do want to
Q return errors if they happen. In this case, the only purpose of this function is
/ to read the line and print it, so we use `()`.
 ## Platform-specific behavior
N Many I/O functions throughout the standard library are documented to indicate
M what various library or syscalls they are delegated to. This is done to help
T applications both understand what's happening under the hood as well as investigate
W any possibly unclear semantics. Note, however, that this is informative, not a binding
S contract. The implementation of many of these functions are subject to change over
< time and may call fewer or more syscalls/library functions.
 ## I/O Safety
_ Rust follows an I/O safety discipline that is comparable to its memory safety discipline. This
] means that file descriptors can be *exclusively owned*. (Here, "file descriptor" is meant to
` subsume similar concepts that exist across a wide range of operating systems even if they might
] use a different name, such as "handle".) An exclusively owned file descriptor is one that no
^ other code is allowed to access in any way, but the owner is allowed to access and even close
X it any time. A type that owns its file descriptor should usually close it in its `drop`
U function. Types like [`File`] own their file descriptor. Similarly, file descriptors
_ can be *borrowed*, granting the temporary right to perform operations on this file descriptor.
_ This indicates that the file descriptor will not be closed for the lifetime of the borrow, but
^ it does *not* imply any right to close this file descriptor, since it will likely be owned by
 someone else.
Y The platform-specific parts of the Rust standard library expose types that reflect these
0 concepts, see [`os::unix`] and [`os::windows`].
] To uphold I/O safety, it is crucial that no code acts on file descriptors it does not own or
] borrow, and no code closes file descriptors it does not own. In other words, a safe function
\ that takes a regular integer, treats it as a file descriptor, and acts on it, is *unsound*.
` Not upholding I/O safety and acting on a file descriptor without proof of ownership can lead to
U misbehavior and even Undefined Behavior in code that relies on ownership of its file
` descriptors: a closed file descriptor could be re-allocated, so the original owner of that file
^ descriptor is now working on the wrong file. Some code might even rely on fully encapsulating
Z its file descriptors with no operations being performed by any other part of the program.
_ Note that exclusive ownership of a file descriptor does *not* imply exclusive ownership of the
e underlying kernel object that the file descriptor references (also called "open file description" on
a some operating systems). File descriptors basically work like [`Arc`]: when you receive an owned
a file descriptor, you cannot know whether there are any other file descriptors that reference the
` same kernel object. However, when you create a new kernel object, you know that you are holding
\ the only reference to it. Just be careful not to lend it to anyone, since they can obtain a
a clone and then you can no longer know what the reference count is! In that sense, [`OwnedFd`] is
\ like `Arc` and [`BorrowedFd<'a>`] is like `&'a Arc` (and similar for the Windows types). In
_ particular, given a `BorrowedFd<'a>`, you are not allowed to close the file descriptor -- just
V like how, given a `&'a Arc`, you are not allowed to decrement the reference count and
` potentially free the underlying object. There is no equivalent to `Box` for file descriptors in
] the standard library (that would be a type that guarantees that the reference count is `1`),
Q however, it would be possible for a crate to define a type with those semantics.
 [`File`]: crate::fs::File

8 The `BufReader<R>` struct adds buffering to any reader.
M It can be excessively inefficient to work directly with a [`Read`] instance.
H For example, every call to [`read`][`TcpStream::read`] on [`TcpStream`]
O results in a system call. A `BufReader<R>` performs large, infrequent reads on
J the underlying [`Read`] and maintains an in-memory buffer of the results.
G `BufReader<R>` can improve the speed of programs that make *small* and
F *repeated* read calls to the same file or network socket. It does not
K help when reading very large amounts at once, or reading just one or a few
H times. It also provides no advantage when reading from a source that is
3 already in memory, like a <code>[Vec]\<u8></code>.
G When the `BufReader<R>` is dropped, the contents of its buffer will be
G discarded. Creating multiple instances of a `BufReader<R>` on the same
E stream can cause data loss. Reading from the underlying reader after
L unwrapping the `BufReader<R>` with [`BufReader::into_inner`] can also cause
 data loss.
1 [`TcpStream::read`]: crate::net::TcpStream::read
$     let f = File::open("log.txt")?;

7 Seek to an offset, in bytes, in the underlying reader.
O The position used for seeking with <code>[SeekFrom::Current]\(_)</code> is the
H position the underlying reader would be at if the `BufReader<R>` had no

G Seeking always discards the internal buffer, even if the seek position
= would otherwise fall within it. This guarantees that calling
R [`BufReader::into_inner()`] immediately after a seek yields the underlying reader

( See [`std::io::Seek`] for more details.
V Note: In the edge case where you're seeking with <code>[SeekFrom::Current]\(n)</code>
C where `n` minus the internal buffer length overflows an `i64`, two
C seeks will be performed instead of one. If the second seek returns
J [`Err`], the underlying reader will be left at the same position it would
E have if you called `seek` with <code>[SeekFrom::Current]\(0)</code>.

G It can be excessively inefficient to work directly with something that

P `BufWriter<W>` keeps an in-memory buffer of data and writes it to an underlying
% writer in large, infrequent batches.
G `BufWriter<W>` can improve the speed of programs that make *small* and

K together by the buffer and will all be written out in one system call when

 Creates an anonymous pipe.
 # Behavior
] A pipe is a one-way data channel provided by the OS, which works across processes. A pipe is
[ typically used to communicate between two or more separate processes, as there are better,
4 faster ways to communicate within a single process.
 In particular:
A * A read on a [`PipeReader`] blocks until the pipe is non-empty.
< * A write on a [`PipeWriter`] blocks when the pipe is full.
] * When all copies of a [`PipeWriter`] are closed, a read on the corresponding [`PipeReader`]
   returns EOF.
_ * [`PipeWriter`] can be shared, and multiple processes or threads can write to it at once, but
N   writes (above a target-specific threshold) may have their data interleaved.
[ * [`PipeReader`] can be shared, and multiple processes or threads can read it at once. Any
V   given byte will only get consumed by one reader. There are no guarantees about data
   interleaving.
[ * Portable applications cannot assume any atomicity of messages larger than a single byte.
K This function currently corresponds to the `pipe` function on Unix and the
" `CreatePipe` function on Windows.
 # Capacity
D Pipe capacity is platform dependent. To quote the Linux [man page]:
] > Different implementations have different limits for the pipe capacity. Applications should
a > not rely on a particular capacity: an application should be designed so that a reading process
_ > consumes data as soon as it is available, so that a writing process does not remain blocked.
% # fn main() -> std::io::Result<()> {
" use std::io::{Read, Write, pipe};
 use std::process::Command;
. let (ping_reader, mut ping_writer) = pipe()?;
. let (mut pong_reader, pong_writer) = pipe()?;
0 // Spawn a child process that echoes its input.
, let mut echo_command = Command::new("cat");
! echo_command.stdin(ping_reader);
" echo_command.stdout(pong_writer);
, let mut echo_child = echo_command.spawn()?;
\ // Send input to the child process. Note that because we're writing all the input before we
Z // read any output, this could deadlock if the child's input and output pipe buffers both
^ // filled up. Those buffers are usually at least a few KB, so "hello" is fine, but for longer
L // inputs we'd need to read and write at the same time, e.g. using threads.
" ping_writer.write_all(b"hello")?;
Y // `cat` exits when it reads EOF from stdin, but that can't happen while any ping writer
Y // remains open. We need to drop our ping writer, or read_to_string will deadlock below.
 drop(ping_writer);
^ // The pong reader can't report EOF while any pong writer remains open. Our Command object is
V // holding a pong writer, and again read_to_string will deadlock if we don't drop it.
 drop(echo_command);
 let mut buf = String::new();
8 // Block until `cat` closes its stdout (a pong writer).
' pong_reader.read_to_string(&mut buf)?;
 assert_eq!(&buf, "hello");
_ // At this point we know `cat` has exited, but we still need to wait to clean up the "zombie".
 echo_child.wait()?;
	 # Ok(())
> [man page]: https://man7.org/linux/man-pages/man7/pipe.7.html
  }T

9 The `Read` trait allows for reading bytes from a source.
7 Implementors of the `Read` trait are called 'readers'.
P Readers are defined by one required method, [`read()`]. Each call to [`read()`]
F will attempt to pull bytes from this source into a provided buffer. A
G number of other methods are implemented in terms of [`read()`], giving
L implementors a number of ways to read bytes while only needing to implement
 a single method.
J Readers are intended to be composable with one another. Many implementors
I throughout [`std::io`] take and provide types which implement the `Read`
 trait.
H Please note that each call to [`read()`] may involve a system call, and
@ therefore, using something that implements [`BufRead`], such as
' [`BufReader`], will be more efficient.

F Pull some bytes from this source into the specified buffer, returning
 how many bytes were read.
F This function does not provide any guarantees about whether it blocks
I waiting for data, but if an object needs to block for a read and cannot,
; it will typically signal this via an [`Err`] return value.
K If the return value of this method is [`Ok(n)`], then implementations must
D guarantee that `0 <= n <= buf.len()`. A nonzero `n` value indicates
J that the buffer `buf` has been filled in with `n` bytes of data from this
B source. If `n` is `0`, then it can indicate one of two scenarios:
G 1. This reader has reached its "end of file" and will likely no longer
C    be able to produce bytes. Note that this does not mean that the
K    reader will *always* no longer be able to produce bytes. As an example,
K    on Linux, this method will call the `recv` syscall for a [`TcpStream`],
P    where returning zero indicates the connection was shut down correctly. While
Q    for [`File`], it is possible to reach the end of file and get zero as result,
P    but if more data is appended to the file, future calls to `read` will return
    more data.
/ 2. The buffer specified was 0 bytes in length.
N It is not an error if the returned value `n` is smaller than the buffer size,
: even when the reader is not at the end of the stream yet.
Q This may happen for example because fewer bytes are actually available right now
R (e. g. being close to end-of-file) or because read() was interrupted by a signal.
J As this trait is safe to implement, callers in unsafe code cannot rely on
 `n <= buf.len()` for safety.
X Extra care needs to be taken when `unsafe` functions are used to access the read bytes.
U Callers have to ensure that no unchecked out-of-bounds accesses are possible even if
 `n > buf.len()`.
Z *Implementations* of this method can make no assumptions about the contents of `buf` when
Y this function is called. It is recommended that implementations only write data to `buf`
! instead of reading its contents.
R Correspondingly, however, *callers* of this method in unsafe code must not assume
X any guarantees about how the implementation uses `buf`. The trait is safe to implement,
W so it is possible that the code that's supposed to write to the buffer might also read
J from it. It is your responsibility to make sure that `buf` is initialized
S before calling `read`. Calling `read` with an uninitialized `buf` (of the kind one
Q obtains via [`MaybeUninit<T>`]) is not safe, and can lead to undefined behavior.
, [`MaybeUninit<T>`]: crate::mem::MaybeUninit

B variant will be returned. If an error is returned then it must be

H This is a convenience function for [`Read::read_to_string`]. Using this
I function avoids having to create a variable first and provides more type
N safety since you can only get the buffer out if there were no errors. (If you
L use [`Read::read_to_string`] you have to remember to check whether the read
O succeeded because otherwise your buffer will be empty or only partially full.)
I The downside of this function's increased ease of use and type safety is
H that it gives you less control over performance. For example, you can't

H [`Read::read_to_string`]. Also, you can't re-use the buffer if an error

O and type safety tradeoffs will be worth it. However, there are cases where you

1 Networking primitives for TCP/UDP communication.
T This module provides networking functionality for the Transmission Control and User
W Datagram Protocols, as well as types for IP and socket addresses and functions related
 to network properties.
 # Organization
U * [`TcpListener`] and [`TcpStream`] provide functionality for communication over TCP
B * [`UdpSocket`] provides functionality for communication over UDP
N * [`IpAddr`] represents IP addresses of either IPv4 or IPv6; [`Ipv4Addr`] and
8   [`Ipv6Addr`] are respectively IPv4 and IPv6 addresses
V * [`SocketAddr`] represents socket addresses of either IPv4 or IPv6; [`SocketAddrV4`]
G   and [`SocketAddrV6`] are respectively IPv4 and IPv6 socket addresses
\ * [`ToSocketAddrs`] is a trait that is used for generic address resolution when interacting
O   with networking objects like [`TcpListener`], [`TcpStream`] or [`UdpSocket`]
O * Other types are return or parameter types for various methods in this module
^ Rust disables inheritance of socket objects to child processes by default when possible.  For
\ example, through the use of the `CLOEXEC` flag in UNIX systems or the `HANDLE_FLAG_INHERIT`
 flag on Windows.

F A trait for objects which can be converted or resolved to one or more
 [`SocketAddr`] values.
L This trait is used for generic address resolution when constructing network
? objects. By default it is implemented for the following types:
A  * [`SocketAddr`]: [`to_socket_addrs`] is the identity function.
G  * [`SocketAddrV4`], [`SocketAddrV6`], <code>([IpAddr], [u16])</code>,
G    <code>([Ipv4Addr], [u16])</code>, <code>([Ipv6Addr], [u16])</code>:
>    [`to_socket_addrs`] constructs a [`SocketAddr`] trivially.
^  * <code>(&[str], [u16])</code>: <code>&[str]</code> should be either a string representation
P    of an [`IpAddr`] address as expected by [`FromStr`] implementation or a host
%    name. [`u16`] is the port number.
Q  * <code>&[str]</code>: the string should be either a string representation of a
Q    [`SocketAddr`] as expected by its [`FromStr`] implementation or a string like
@    `<host_name>:<port>` pair where `<port>` is a [`u16`] value.
V  * <code>&[[SocketAddr]]</code>: all [`SocketAddr`] values in the slice will be used.
E This trait allows constructing network objects like [`TcpStream`] or
J [`UdpSocket`] easily with values of various types for the bind/connection
J address. It is needed because sometimes one type is more appropriate than
K the other: for simple uses a string like `"localhost:12345"` is much nicer
L than manual construction of the corresponding [`SocketAddr`], but sometimes
O [`SocketAddr`] value is *the* main source of the address, and converting it to
E some other type (e.g., a string) just for it to be converted back to
4 [`SocketAddr`] in constructor methods is pointless.
I Addresses returned by the operating system that are not IP addresses are
 silently ignored.

4 Unix-specific extensions to general I/O primitives.
E Just like raw pointers, raw file descriptors point to resources with
G dynamic lifetimes, and they can dangle if they outlive their resources
5 or be forged if they're created from invalid values.
D This module provides three types for representing file descriptors,
I with different ownership properties: raw, borrowed, and owned, which are
X analogous to types used for representing pointers. These types reflect concepts of [I/O
 safety][io-safety] on Unix.
& | Type               | Analogous to |
& | ------------------ | ------------ |
& | [`RawFd`]          | `*const _`   |
& | [`BorrowedFd<'a>`] | `&'a Arc<_>` |
& | [`OwnedFd`]        | `Arc<_>`     |
I Like raw pointers, `RawFd` values are primitive values. And in new code,
J they should be considered unsafe to do I/O on (analogous to dereferencing
J them). Rust did not always provide this guidance, so existing code in the
; Rust ecosystem often doesn't mark `RawFd` usage as unsafe.
% Libraries are encouraged to migrate,
I either by adding `unsafe` to APIs that dereference `RawFd` values, or by
, using to `BorrowedFd` or `OwnedFd` instead.
^ The use of `Arc` for borrowed/owned file descriptors may be surprising. Unix file descriptors
] are mere references to internal kernel objects called "open file descriptions", and the same
^ open file description can be referenced by multiple file descriptors (e.g. if `dup` is used).
` State such as the offset within the file is shared among all file descriptors that refer to the
` same open file description, and the kernel internally does reference-counting to only close the
_ underlying resource once all file descriptors referencing it are closed. That's why `Arc` (and
F not `Box`) is the closest Rust analogy to an "owned" file descriptor.
G Like references, `BorrowedFd` values are tied to a lifetime, to ensure
F that they don't outlive the resource they point to. These are safe to
J use. `BorrowedFd` values may be used in APIs which provide safe access to
 any system call except for:
H  - `close`, because that would end the dynamic lifetime of the resource
F    without ending the lifetime of the file descriptor. (Equivalently:
%    an `&Arc<_>` cannot be `drop`ed.)
C  - `dup2`/`dup3`, in the second argument, because this argument is
J    closed and assigned a new resource, which may break the assumptions of
*    other code using that file descriptor.
a `BorrowedFd` values may be used in APIs which provide safe access to `dup` system calls, so code
Z working with `OwnedFd` cannot assume to have exclusive access to the underlying open file
_ description. (Equivalently: `&Arc` may be used in APIs that provide safe access to `clone`, so
I code working with an `Arc` cannot assume that the reference count is 1.)
H `BorrowedFd` values may also be used with `mmap`, since `mmap` uses the
K provided file descriptor in a manner similar to `dup` and does not require
H the `BorrowedFd` passed to it to live for the lifetime of the resulting
K mapping. That said, `mmap` is unsafe for other reasons: it operates on raw
J pointers, and it can have undefined behavior if the underlying storage is
K mutated. Mutations may come from other processes, or from the same process
E if the API provides `BorrowedFd` access, since as mentioned earlier,
I `BorrowedFd` values may be used in APIs which provide safe access to any
L system call. Consequently, code using `mmap` and presenting a safe API must
G take full responsibility for ensuring that safe Rust code cannot evoke
 undefined behavior through it.
[ Like `Arc`, `OwnedFd` values conceptually own one reference to the resource they point to,
N and decrement the reference count when they are dropped (by calling `close`).
W When the reference count reaches 0, the underlying open file description will be freed
 by the kernel.
O See the [`io` module docs][io-safety] for a general explanation of I/O safety.
, ## `/proc/self/mem` and similar OS features
C Some platforms have special files, such as `/proc/self/mem`, which
B provide read and write access to the process's memory. Such reads
K and writes happen outside the control of the Rust compiler, so they do not
( uphold Rust's memory safety guarantees.
C This does not mean that all APIs that might allow `/proc/self/mem`
Q to be opened and read from or written must be `unsafe`. Rust's safety guarantees
I only cover what the program itself can do, and not what entities outside
G the program can do to it. `/proc/self/mem` is considered to be such an
^ external entity, along with `/proc/self/fd/*`, debugging interfaces, and people with physical
a access to the hardware. This is true even in cases where the program is controlling the external
 entity.
H If you desire to comprehensively prevent programs from reaching out and
K causing external entities to reach back in and violate memory safety, it's
D necessary to use *sandboxing*, which is outside the scope of `std`.
4 [`BorrowedFd<'a>`]: crate::os::unix::io::BorrowedFd
! [io-safety]: crate::io#io-safety

 # `SOCK_CLOEXEC`
a On platforms that support it, we pass the close-on-exec flag to atomically create the socket and
_ set it as CLOEXEC. On Linux, this was added in 2.6.27. See [`socket(2)`] for more information.

 # Notes and Safety
E This closure will be run in the context of the child process after a
F `fork`. This primarily means that any modifications made to memory on
F behalf of this closure will **not** be visible to the parent process.
E This is often a very constrained environment where normal operations
D like `malloc`, accessing environment variables through [`std::env`]
8 or acquiring a mutex are not guaranteed to work (due to
> other threads perhaps still running when the `fork` was run).
G Note that the list of allocating functions includes [`Error::new`] and
D [`Error::other`]. To signal a non-trivial error, prefer [`panic!`].
> For further details refer to the [POSIX fork() specification]
2 and the equivalent documentation for any targeted
D platform, especially the requirements around *async-signal-safety*.
@ This also means that all resources such as file descriptors and
H memory-mapped regions got duplicated. It is your responsibility to make
D sure that the closure does not violate library invariants by making
! invalid use of these duplicates.
J Panicking in the closure is safe only if all the format arguments for the
@ panic message can be safely formatted; this is because although
I `Command` calls [`std::panic::always_abort`](crate::panic::always_abort)
E before calling the pre_exec hook, panic will still try to format the
 panic message.
I When this closure is run, aspects such as the stdio file descriptors and
E working directory have successfully been changed, so output to these
+ locations might not appear where intended.
 [POSIX fork() specification]:
I     https://pubs.opengroup.org/onlinepubs/9699919799/functions/fork.html
 [`std::env`]: mod@crate::env
& [`Error::new`]: crate::io::Error::new
* [`Error::other`]: crate::io::Error::other
] `before_exec` used to be a safe method, but it needs to be unsafe since the closure may only
Y perform operations that are *async-signal-safe*. Hence it got deprecated in favor of the
X unsafe [`pre_exec`]. Meanwhile, Rust gained the ability to make an existing safe method
Y fully unsafe in a new edition, which is how `before_exec` became `unsafe`. It still also
7 remains deprecated; `pre_exec` should be used instead.
# [`pre_exec`]: CommandExt::pre_exec
(should be unsafe, use `pre_exec` instead

G [`process::exit`]

J The file descriptor for the standard input stream of the current process.
_ See [`io::stdin()`][`crate::io::stdin`] for the higher level handle, which should be preferred
] whenever possible. See [`STDERR`] for why the file descriptor might be required and caveats.
K The file descriptor for the standard output stream of the current process.
a See [`io::stdout()`][`crate::io::stdout`] for the higher level handle, which should be preferred
` whenever possible. See [`STDERR`] for why the file descriptor might be required and caveats. In
a addition to the issues discussed there, note that [`Stdout`][`crate::io::Stdout`] is buffered by
E default, and writing to the file descriptor will bypass this buffer.
J The file descriptor for the standard error stream of the current process.
a See [`io::stderr()`][`crate::io::stderr`] for the higher level handle, which should be preferred
_ whenever possible. However, there are situations where touching the `std::io` handles (or most
X other parts of the standard library) risks deadlocks or other subtle bugs. For example:
\ - Global allocators must be careful to [avoid reentrancy][global-alloc-reentrancy], and the
<   `std::io` handles may allocate memory on (some) accesses.
\ - Signal handlers must be *async-signal-safe*, which rules out panicking, taking locks (may
`   deadlock if the signal handler interrupted a thread holding that lock), allocating memory, or
C   anything else that is not explicitly declared async-signal-safe.
` - `CommandExt::pre_exec` callbacks can safely panic (with some limitations), but otherwise must
^   abide by similar limitations as signal handlers. In particular, at the time these callbacks
[   run, the stdio file descriptors have already been replaced, but the locks protecting the
`   `std::io` handles may be permanently locked if another thread held the lock at `fork()` time.
^ In these and similar cases, direct access to the file descriptor may be required. However, in
Y most cases, using the `std::io` handles and accessing the file descriptor via the `AsFd`
a implementations is preferable, as it enables cooperation with the standard library's locking and
 buffering.
 # I/O safety
[ This is a `BorrowedFd<'static>` because the standard input/output/error streams are shared
] resources that must remain available for the lifetime of the process. This is only true when
` linking `std`, and may not always hold for [code running before `main()`][before-after-main] or
[ in `no_std` environments. It is [unsound][io-safety] to close these file descriptors. Safe
` patterns for changing these file descriptors are available on Unix via the `StdioExt` extension
G [before-after-main]: ../../../std/index.html#use-before-and-after-main
2 [io-safety]: ../../../std/io/index.html#io-safety
Q [global-alloc-reentrancy]: ../../../std/alloc/trait.GlobalAlloc.html#re-entrance
0 Platform-specific extensions to [`SocketAddr`].
9 Creates a Unix socket address in the abstract namespace.
F The abstract namespace is a Linux-specific extension that allows Unix
A sockets to be bound without creating an entry in the filesystem.
E Abstract sockets are unaffected by filesystem layout or permissions,
7 and no cleanup is necessary when the socket is closed.
G An abstract socket address name may contain any bytes, including zero.
; Returns an error if the name is longer than `SUN_LEN - 1`.

L Invokes a closure, capturing the cause of an unwinding panic if one occurs.
M This function will return `Ok` with the closure's result if the closure does

@ returned is the object with which panic was originally invoked.
J Rust functions that are expected to be called from foreign code that does
L not support unwinding (such as C compiled with `-fno-exceptions`) should be
K defined using `extern "C"`, which ensures that if the Rust code panics, it
K is automatically caught and the process is aborted. If this is the desired
E behavior, it is not necessary to use `catch_unwind` explicitly. This
M function should instead be used when more graceful error-handling is needed.
G It is **not** recommended to use this function for a general try/catch
M mechanism. The [`Result`] type is more appropriate to use for functions that

4 to catch all panics, see the "Notes" section below.
J The closure provided is required to adhere to the [`UnwindSafe`] trait to
H ensure that all captured variables are safe to cross this boundary. The
M purpose of this bound is to encode the concept of [exception safety][rfc] in
L the type system. Most usage of this function should not need to worry about
K this bound as programs are naturally unwind safe without `unsafe` code. If
L it becomes a problem the [`AssertUnwindSafe`] wrapper struct can be used to
: quickly assert that the usage here is indeed unwind safe.

 # Use after fork
Y This function is particularly useful for calling after `libc::fork`.  After `fork`, in a
\ multithreaded program it is (on many platforms) not safe to call the allocator.  It is also
^ generally highly undesirable for an unwind to unwind past the `fork`, because that results in
R the unwind propagating to code that was only ever expecting to run in the parent.
^ `panic::always_abort()` helps avoid both of these.  It directly avoids any further unwinding,
` and if there is a panic, the abort will occur without allocating provided that the arguments to
+ panic can be formatted without allocating.
  #![feature(panic_always_abort)]

5 Helper module for exporting the `pattern_type` macro
" Cross-platform path manipulation.
M This module provides two types, [`PathBuf`] and [`Path`] (akin to [`String`]
O and [`str`]), for working with paths abstractly. These types are thin wrappers
P around [`OsString`] and [`OsStr`] respectively, meaning that they work directly
: on strings according to the local platform's path syntax.
H Paths can be parsed into [`Component`]s by iterating over the structure
J returned by the [`components`] method on [`Path`]. [`Component`]s roughly
K correspond to the substrings between path separators (`/` or `\`). You can
K reconstruct an equivalent path from components with the [`push`] method on
A [`PathBuf`]; note that the paths may differ syntactically by the
L normalization described in the documentation for the [`components`] method.
 ## Case sensitivity
K Unless otherwise indicated path methods that do not access the filesystem,
M such as [`Path::starts_with`] and [`Path::ends_with`], are case sensitive no
L matter the platform or filesystem. An exception to this is made for Windows
 drive letters.
 ## Path normalization
P Several methods in this module perform basic path normalization by disregarding
Y repeated separators, non-leading `.` components, and trailing separators. These include:
I - Methods for iteration, such as [`Path::components`] and [`Path::iter`]
5 - Methods for inspection, such as [`Path::has_root`]
? - Comparisons using [`PartialEq`], [`PartialOrd`], and [`Ord`]
F [`Path::join`] and [`PathBuf::push`] also disregard trailing slashes.
U These methods **do not** resolve `..` components or symlinks. For full normalization
Z including `..` resolution, use [`Path::canonicalize`] (which does access the filesystem).
 ## Simple usage
L Path manipulation includes both parsing components from slices and building
 new owned paths.
@ To parse a path, you can create a [`Path`] slice from a [`str`]
" slice and start asking questions:
* let path = Path::new("/tmp/foo/bar.txt");
 let parent = path.parent();
1 assert_eq!(parent, Some(Path::new("/tmp/foo")));
" let file_stem = path.file_stem();
0 assert_eq!(file_stem, Some(OsStr::new("bar")));
" let extension = path.extension();
0 assert_eq!(extension, Some(OsStr::new("txt")));

A When parsing the path, there is a small amount of normalization:
A * Repeated separators are ignored, so `a/b` and `a//b` both have
   `a` and `b` as components.
D * Occurrences of `.` are normalized away, except if they are at the
C   beginning of the path. For example, `a/./b`, `a/b/`, `a/b/.` and

&   an additional [`CurDir`] component.
Q * Trailing separators are normalized away, so `/a/b` and `/a/b/` are equivalent.
C Note that no other normalization takes place; in particular, `a/c`
E and `a/b/../c` are distinct, to account for the possibility that `b`

% A module for working with processes.
I This module is mostly concerned with spawning and interacting with child
K processes, but it also provides [`abort`] and [`exit`] for terminating the
 current process.
 # Spawning a process
A The [`Command`] struct is used to configure and spawn processes:
" let output = Command::new("echo")
     .arg("Hello world")
     .output()
*     .expect("Failed to execute command");
8 assert_eq!(b"Hello world\n", output.stdout.as_slice());
M Several methods on [`Command`], such as [`spawn`] or [`output`], can be used
K to spawn a process. In particular, [`output`] spawns the child process and
L waits until the process terminates, while [`spawn`] will return a [`Child`]
+ that represents the spawned child process.
 # Handling I/O
D The [`stdout`], [`stdin`], and [`stderr`] of a child process can be
B configured by passing an [`Stdio`] to the corresponding method on
H [`Command`]. Once spawned, they can be accessed from the [`Child`]. For
I example, piping output from one command into another command can be done
	 like so:
$ use std::process::{Command, Stdio};
A // stdout must be configured with `Stdio::piped` in order to use
 // `echo_child.stdout`
& let echo_child = Command::new("echo")
     .arg("Oh no, a tpyo!")
     .stdout(Stdio::piped())
     .spawn()
-     .expect("Failed to start echo process");
A // Note that `echo_child` is moved here, but we won't be needing
 // `echo_child` anymore
G let echo_out = echo_child.stdout.expect("Failed to open echo stdout");
( let mut sed_child = Command::new("sed")
     .arg("s/tpyo/typo/")
"     .stdin(Stdio::from(echo_out))
,     .expect("Failed to start sed process");
K let output = sed_child.wait_with_output().expect("Failed to wait on sed");
; assert_eq!(b"Oh no, a typo!\n", output.stdout.as_slice());
E Note that [`ChildStderr`] and [`ChildStdout`] implement [`Read`] and
% [`ChildStdin`] implements [`Write`]:
) let mut child = Command::new("/bin/cat")
     .stdin(Stdio::piped())
(     .expect("failed to execute child");
? // If the child process fills its stdout buffer, it may end up
A // waiting until the parent reads the stdout, and not be able to
3 // read stdin in the meantime, causing a deadlock.
A // Writing from another thread ensures that stdout is being read
+ // at the same time, avoiding the problem.
B let mut stdin = child.stdin.take().expect("failed to get stdin");
 std::thread::spawn(move || {
A     stdin.write_all(b"test").expect("failed to write to stdin");
 let output = child
     .wait_with_output()
(     .expect("failed to wait on child");
/ assert_eq!(b"test", output.stdout.as_slice());
 # Windows argument splitting
N On Unix systems arguments are passed to a new process as an array of strings,
M but on Windows arguments are passed as a single commandline string and it is
L up to the child process to parse it into an array. Therefore the parent and
E child processes must agree on how the commandline string is encoded.
L Most programs use the standard C run-time `argv`, which in practice results
N in consistent argument handling. However, some programs have their own way of
M parsing the commandline string. In these cases using [`arg`] or [`args`] may
K result in the child process seeing a different array of arguments than the
 parent process intended.
! Two ways of mitigating this are:
B * Validate untrusted input so that only a safe subset is allowed.
L * Use [`raw_arg`] to build a custom commandline. This bypasses the escaping
<   rules used by [`arg`] so should be used with due caution.
P `cmd.exe` and `.bat` files use non-standard argument parsing and are especially
I vulnerable to malicious input as they may be used to run arbitrary shell
H commands. Untrusted arguments should be restricted as much as possible.
/ For examples on handling this see [`raw_arg`].
  ### Batch file special handling
J On Windows, `Command` uses the Windows API function [`CreateProcessW`] to
F spawn new processes. An undocumented feature of this function is that
J when given a `.bat` file as the application to run, it will automatically
Q convert that into running `cmd.exe /c` with the batch file as the next argument.
I For historical reasons Rust currently preserves this behavior when using
J [`Command::new`], and escapes the arguments according to `cmd.exe` rules.
F Due to the complexity of `cmd.exe` argument handling, it might not be
N possible to safely escape some special characters, and using them will result
E in an error being returned at process spawn. The set of unescapeable
2 special characters might change between releases.
G Also note that running batch scripts in this way may be removed in the
) future and so should not be relied upon.

~ [`CreateProcessW`]: https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/api/processthreadsapi/nf-processthreadsapi-createprocessw
5 Representation of a running or exited child process.
H This structure is used to represent and manage child processes. A child
D process is created via the [`Command`] struct, which configures the
E spawning process and can itself be constructed using a builder-style
 interface.
< There is no implementation of [`Drop`] for child processes,
H so if you do not ensure the `Child` has exited then it will continue to
H run, even after the `Child` handle to the child process has gone out of
D Calling [`wait`] (or other functions that wrap around it) will make
C the parent process wait until the child has actually exited before
 continuing.
 # Warning
H On some systems, calling [`wait`] or similar is necessary for the OS to
K release resources. A process that terminated but has not been waited on is
H still around as a "zombie". Leaving too many zombies around may exhaust
, global resources (for example process IDs).
K The standard library does *not* automatically wait on child processes (not
M even if the `Child` is dropped), it is up to the application developer to do
M so. As a consequence, dropping `Child` handles without waiting on them first
1 is not recommended in long-running applications.
     .arg("file.txt")
< let ecode = child.wait().expect("failed to wait on child");
 assert!(ecode.success());
 [`wait`]: Child::wait

2 A process builder, providing fine-grained control
* over how a new process should be spawned.
 A default configuration can be
M generated using `Command::new(program)`, where `program` gives a path to the
K program to be executed. Additional builder methods allow the configuration
D to be changed (for example, by adding arguments) prior to spawning:
I # if cfg!(not(all(target_vendor = "apple", not(target_os = "macos")))) {
. let output = if cfg!(target_os = "windows") {
     Command::new("cmd")
$         .args(["/C", "echo hello"])
         .output()
-         .expect("failed to execute process")
     Command::new("sh")
         .arg("-c")
         .arg("echo hello")
 let hello = output.stdout;
I `Command` can be reused to spawn multiple processes. The builder methods
E change the command without needing to immediately spawn the process.
) let mut echo_hello = Command::new("sh");
( echo_hello.arg("-c").arg("echo hello");
G let hello_1 = echo_hello.output().expect("failed to execute process");
G let hello_2 = echo_hello.output().expect("failed to execute process");
J Similarly, you can call builder methods after spawning a process and then
0 spawn a new process with the modified settings.
' let mut list_dir = Command::new("ls");
9 // Execute `ls` in the current directory of the program.
7 list_dir.status().expect("process failed to execute");
 println!();
1 // Change `ls` to execute in the root directory.
 list_dir.current_dir("/");
: // And then execute `ls` again but in the root directory.
8 Constructs a new `Command` for launching the program at
: path `program`, with the following default configuration:
 * No arguments to the program
, * Inherit the current process's environment
2 * Inherit the current process's working directory
[ * Inherit stdin/stdout/stderr for [`spawn`] or [`status`], but create pipes for [`output`]
 [`spawn`]: Self::spawn
 [`status`]: Self::status
 [`output`]: Self::output
: Builder methods are provided to change these defaults and
! otherwise configure the process.
F If `program` is not an absolute path, the `PATH` environment variable
' will be searched in an OS-defined way.
C The details below describe the current behavior, but these details
' may change in future versions of Rust.
A On Unix, the `PATH` searched comes from the child's environment:
D - If the environment is unmodified, the child inherits the parent's
'   `PATH` and that is what is searched.
G - If `PATH` is explicitly set via [`env`], that new value is searched.
> - If [`env_clear`] or [`env_remove`] removes `PATH` without a
G   replacement, `execvp` falls back to an OS-defined default (typically
G   `/bin:/usr/bin`), **not** the parent's `PATH`. This may fail to find
-   programs that rely on the parent's `PATH`.
E To avoid surprises, use an absolute path or explicitly set `PATH` on
6 the `Command` when modifying the child's environment.
F On Windows, Rust resolves the executable path before spawning, rather
? than passing the name to `CreateProcessW` for resolution. When
H `program` is not an absolute path, the following locations are searched
 in order:
6 1. The child's `PATH`, if explicitly set via [`env`].
, 2. The directory of the current executable.
1 3. The system directory (`GetSystemDirectoryW`).
3 4. The Windows directory (`GetWindowsDirectoryW`).
  5. The parent process's `PATH`.
D Note: when `PATH` is cleared via [`env_clear`] or [`env_remove`] on
D Windows, step 1 is skipped but the parent process's `PATH` is still
$ searched at step 5, unlike on Unix.
F For executable files, the `.exe` extension may be omitted. Files with
H other extensions must include the extension, otherwise they will not be
: found. Note that this behavior has some known limitations
 (see issue #37519).
 [`env`]: Self::env
! [`env_remove`]: Self::env_remove
 [`env_clear`]: Self::env_clear

a In its current implementation, this function will execute exit handlers registered with `atexit`
_ as well as other platform-specific exit handlers (e.g. `fini` sections of ELF shared objects).
Y This means that Rust requires that all exit handlers are safe to execute at any time. In
a particular, if an exit handler cleans up some state that might be concurrently accessed by other
[ threads, it is required that the exit handler performs suitable synchronization with those
a threads. (The alternative to this requirement would be to not run exit handlers at all, which is
_ considered undesirable. Note that returning from `main` also calls `exit`, so making `exit` an
$ unsafe operation is not an option.)
L **Unix**: On Unix-like platforms, it is unlikely that all 32 bits of `exit`
F will be visible to a parent process inspecting the exit code. On most
K Unix-like platforms, only the eight least-significant bits are considered.
L For example, the exit code for this example will be `0` on Linux, but `256`
 on Windows:

 process::exit(0x0100);
 ### Safe interop with C code
a On Unix, this function is currently implemented using the `exit` C function [`exit`][C-exit]. As
Z of C23, the C standard does not permit multiple threads to call `exit` concurrently. Rust
a mitigates this with a lock, but if C code calls `exit`, that can still cause undefined behavior.
A Note that returning from `main` is equivalent to calling `exit`.
Y Therefore, it is undefined behavior to have two concurrent threads perform the following
 without synchronization:
Q - One thread calls Rust's `exit` function or returns from Rust's `main` function
b - Another thread calls the C function `exit` or `quick_exit`, or returns from C's `main` function
Y Note that if a binary contains multiple copies of the Rust runtime (e.g., when combining
W multiple `cdylib` or `staticlib`), they each have their own separate lock, so from the
` perspective of code running in one of the Rust runtimes, the "outside" Rust code is basically C
= code, and concurrent `exit` again causes undefined behavior.
c Individual C implementations might provide more guarantees than the standard and permit concurrent
Q calls to `exit`; consult the documentation of your C implementation for details.
J For some of the on-going discussion to make `exit` thread-safe in C, see:
H - [Rust issue #126600](https://github.com/rust-lang/rust/issues/126600)
T - [Austin Group Bugzilla (for POSIX)](https://austingroupbugs.net/view.php?id=1845)
R - [GNU C library Bugzilla](https://sourceware.org/bugzilla/show_bug.cgi?id=31997)
7 [C-exit]: https://en.cppreference.com/w/c/program/exit

# Useful synchronization primitives.
  ## The need for synchronization
B Conceptually, a Rust program is a series of operations which will
C be executed on a computer. The timeline of events happening in the
D program is consistent with the order of the operations in the code.
H Consider the following code, operating on some global static variables:
? // FIXME(static_mut_refs): Do not allow `static_mut_refs` lint
 #![allow(static_mut_refs)]
 static mut A: u32 = 0;
 static mut B: u32 = 0;
 static mut C: u32 = 0;
     unsafe {
         A = 3;
         B = 4;
         A = A + B;
         C = B;
!         println!("{A} {B} {C}");
         C = A;
J It appears as if some variables stored in memory are changed, an addition
> is performed, result is stored in `A` and the variable `C` is
 modified twice.
D When only a single thread is involved, the results are as expected:
 the line `7 4 4` gets printed.
J As for what happens behind the scenes, when optimizations are enabled the
F final generated machine code might look very different from the code:
H - The first store to `C` might be moved before the store to `A` or `B`,
0   _as if_ we had written `C = 4; A = 3; B = 4`.
M - Assignment of `A + B` to `A` might be removed, since the sum can be stored
J   in a temporary location until it gets printed, with the global variable
   never getting updated.
C - The final result could be determined just by looking at the code
>   at compile time, so [constant folding] might turn the whole
+   block into a simple `println!("7 4 4")`.
< The compiler is allowed to perform any combination of these
C optimizations, as long as the final optimized code, when executed,
< produces the same results as the one without optimizations.
C Due to the [concurrency] involved in modern computers, assumptions
? about the program's execution order are often wrong. Access to
C global variables can lead to nondeterministic results, **even if**
B compiler optimizations are disabled, and it is **still possible**
# to introduce synchronization bugs.
H Note that thanks to Rust's safety guarantees, accessing global (static)
C variables requires `unsafe` code, assuming we don't use any of the
+ synchronization primitives in this module.
C [constant folding]: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constant_folding
L [concurrency]: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concurrency_(computer_science)
 ## Out-of-order execution
M Instructions can execute in a different order from the one we define, due to
 various reasons:
I - The **compiler** reordering instructions: If the compiler can issue an
I   instruction at an earlier point, it will try to do so. For example, it
K   might hoist memory loads at the top of a code block, so that the CPU can
.   start [prefetching] the values from memory.
C   In single-threaded scenarios, this can cause issues when writing
6   signal handlers or certain kinds of low-level code.
4   Use [compiler fences] to prevent this reordering.
@ - A **single processor** executing instructions [out-of-order]:
6   Modern CPUs are capable of [superscalar] execution,
C   i.e., multiple instructions might be executing at the same time,
?   even though the machine code describes a sequential process.
?   This kind of reordering is handled transparently by the CPU.
B - A **multiprocessor** system executing multiple hardware threads
A   at the same time: In multi-threaded scenarios, you can use two
4   kinds of primitives to deal with synchronization:
B   - [memory fences] to ensure memory accesses are made visible to
!   other CPUs in the right order.
B   - [atomic operations] to ensure simultaneous access to the same
6   memory location doesn't lead to undefined behavior.
? [prefetching]: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cache_prefetching
7 [compiler fences]: crate::sync::atomic::compiler_fence
E [out-of-order]: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Out-of-order_execution
C [superscalar]: https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superscalar_processor
, [memory fences]: crate::sync::atomic::fence
) [atomic operations]: crate::sync::atomic
( ## Higher-level synchronization objects
K Most of the low-level synchronization primitives are quite error-prone and
I inconvenient to use, which is why the standard library also exposes some
& higher-level synchronization objects.
? These abstractions can be built out of lower-level primitives.
E For efficiency, the sync objects in the standard library are usually
C implemented with help from the operating system's kernel, which is
C able to reschedule the threads while they are blocked on acquiring
 a lock.
> The following is an overview of the available synchronization
	 objects:
C - [`Arc`]: Atomically Reference-Counted pointer, which can be used
@   in multithreaded environments to prolong the lifetime of some
5   data until all the threads have finished using it.
A - [`Barrier`]: Ensures multiple threads will wait for each other
C   to reach a point in the program, before continuing execution all
   together.
B - [`Condvar`]: Condition Variable, providing the ability to block
0   a thread while waiting for an event to occur.
= - [`mpsc`]: Multi-producer, single-consumer queues, used for
9   message-based communication. Can provide a lightweight
>   inter-thread synchronisation mechanism, at the cost of some
   extra memory.
< - [`mpmc`]: Multi-producer, multi-consumer queues, used for
? - [`Mutex`]: Mutual Exclusion mechanism, which ensures that at
9   most one thread at a time is able to access some data.
L - [`Once`]: Used for a thread-safe, one-time global initialization routine.
@   Mostly useful for implementing other types like [`OnceLock`].
C - [`OnceLock`]: Used for thread-safe, one-time initialization of a
I   variable, with potentially different initializers based on the caller.
C - [`LazyLock`]: Used for thread-safe, one-time initialization of a
I   variable, using one nullary initializer function provided at creation.
A - [`RwLock`]: Provides a mutual exclusion mechanism which allows
=   multiple readers at the same time, while allowing only one
C   writer at a time. In some cases, this can be more efficient than
   a mutex.
" [`Barrier`]: crate::sync::Barrier
 [`mpmc`]: crate::sync::mpmc
 [`mpsc`]: crate::sync::mpsc
 [`Once`]: crate::sync::Once
$ [`OnceLock`]: crate::sync::OnceLock
  [`RwLock`]: crate::sync::RwLock

Weak
D Multi-producer, multi-consumer FIFO queue communication primitives.
K This module provides message-based communication over channels, concretely
 defined by two types:
 * [`Sender`]
 * [`Receiver`]
L [`Sender`]s are used to send data to a set of [`Receiver`]s where each item
J sent is delivered to (at most) one receiver. Both sender and receiver are
J cloneable (multi-producer) such that many threads can send simultaneously
 to receivers (multi-consumer).
$ These channels come in two flavors:
J 1. An asynchronous, infinitely buffered channel. The [`channel`] function
D    will return a `(Sender, Receiver)` tuple where all sends will be
H    **asynchronous** (they never block). The channel conceptually has an
    infinite buffer.
F 2. A synchronous, bounded channel. The [`sync_channel`] function will
E    return a `(Sender, Receiver)` tuple where the storage for pending
I    messages is a pre-allocated buffer of a fixed size. All sends will be
K    **synchronous** by blocking until there is buffer space available. Note
N    that a bound of 0 is allowed, causing the channel to become a "rendezvous"
K    channel where each sender atomically hands off a message to a receiver.
 [`send`]: Sender::send
 ## Disconnection
I The send and receive operations on channels will all return a [`Result`]
M indicating whether the operation succeeded or not. An unsuccessful operation
J is normally indicative of the other half of a channel having "hung up" by
+ being dropped in its corresponding thread.
K Once half of a channel has been deallocated, most operations can no longer
J continue to make progress, so [`Err`] will be returned. Many applications
C will continue to [`unwrap`] the results returned from this module,
M instigating a propagation of failure among threads if one unexpectedly dies.
 [`unwrap`]: Result::unwrap
 Simple usage:
 #![feature(mpmc_channel)]
 use std::sync::mpmc::channel;
% // Create a simple streaming channel
     tx.send(10).unwrap();
$ assert_eq!(rx.recv().unwrap(), 10);
 Shared usage:
H     // Create a shared channel that can be sent along from many threads
S     // where tx is the sending half (tx for transmission), and rx is the receiving
      // half (rx for receiving).
     let (tx, rx) = channel();
     for i in 0..10 {
         let tx = tx.clone();
         s.spawn(move || {
!             tx.send(i).unwrap();
         });
     for _ in 0..5 {
         let rx1 = rx.clone();
         let rx2 = rx.clone();
)             let j = rx1.recv().unwrap();
'             assert!(0 <= j && j < 10);
)             let j = rx2.recv().unwrap();
 Propagating panics:
K // The call to recv() will return an error because the channel has already
! // hung up (or been deallocated)
! let (tx, rx) = channel::<i32>();
 drop(tx);
 assert!(rx.recv().is_err());

, Creates a new synchronous, bounded channel.
J All data sent on the [`Sender`] will become available on the [`Receiver`]
F in the same order as it was sent. Like asynchronous [`channel`]s, the
J [`Receiver`] will block until a message becomes available. `sync_channel`
9 differs greatly in the semantics of the sender, however.
F This channel has an internal buffer on which messages will be queued.
J `bound` specifies the buffer size. When the internal buffer becomes full,
I future sends will *block* waiting for the buffer to open up. Note that a
K buffer size of 0 is valid, in which case this becomes "rendezvous channel"
H where each [`send`] will not return until a [`recv`] is paired with it.
F The [`Sender`] can be cloned to [`send`] to the same channel multiple
D times. The [`Receiver`] also can be cloned to have multi receivers.
M Like asynchronous channels, if the [`Receiver`] is disconnected while trying
C to [`send`] with the [`Sender`], the [`send`] method will return a
I [`SendError`]. Similarly, If the [`Sender`] is disconnected while trying
> to [`recv`], the [`recv`] method will return a [`RecvError`].

* let (sender, receiver) = sync_channel(1);

D     // this will block until the previous message has been received
     sender.send(2).unwrap();
) assert_eq!(receiver.recv().unwrap(), 1);
) assert_eq!(receiver.recv().unwrap(), 2);

> to stop blocking to receive messages with [`Receiver::recv`].

> Attempts to send a message into the channel without blocking.
Z This method will either send a message into the channel immediately or return an error if
W the channel is full or disconnected. The returned error contains the original message.
V If called on a zero-capacity channel, this method will send the message only if there
U happens to be a receive operation on the other side of the channel at the same time.
2 use std::sync::mpmc::{channel, Receiver, Sender};
C let (sender, _receiver): (Sender<i32>, Receiver<i32>) = channel();

Z7&D
H Attempts to send a value on this channel, returning it back if it could
 not be sent.
E A successful send occurs when it is determined that the other end of
G the channel has not hung up already. An unsuccessful send would be one
D where the corresponding receiver has already been deallocated. Note
A that a return value of [`Err`] means that the data will never be
E received, but a return value of [`Ok`] does *not* mean that the data
C will be received. It is possible for the corresponding receiver to

R `T` must be `Send` for a [`Mutex`] to be `Send` because it is possible to acquire
3 the owned `T` from the `Mutex` via [`into_inner`].
" [`into_inner`]: Mutex::into_inner
/ `T` must be `Send` for [`Mutex`] to be `Sync`.
R This ensures that the protected data can be accessed safely from multiple threads
5 without causing data races or other unsafe behavior.
] [`Mutex<T>`] provides mutable access to `T` to one thread at a time. However, it's essential
W for `T` to be `Send` because it's not safe for non-`Send` structures to be accessed in
Z this manner. For instance, consider [`Rc`], a non-atomic reference counted smart pointer,
V which is not `Send`. With `Rc`, we can have multiple copies pointing to the same heap
Y allocation with a non-atomic reference count. If we were to use `Mutex<Rc<_>>`, it would
V only protect one instance of `Rc` from shared access, leaving other copies vulnerable
 to potential data races.
W Also note that it is not necessary for `T` to be `Sync` as `&T` is only made available
. to one thread at a time if `T` is not `Sync`.
 [`Rc`]: crate::rc::Rc
A A [`MutexGuard`] is not `Send` to maximize platform portability.
a On platforms that use POSIX threads (commonly referred to as pthreads) there is a requirement to
; release mutex locks on the same thread they were acquired.
[ For this reason, [`MutexGuard`] must not implement `Send` to prevent it being dropped from

8 `T` must be `Sync` for a [`MutexGuard<T>`] to be `Sync`

/ Synchronization objects that employ poisoning.
G All synchronization objects in this module implement a strategy called
J "poisoning" where a primitive becomes poisoned if it recognizes that some
F thread has panicked while holding the exclusive access granted by the
D primitive. This information is then propagated to all other threads
G to signify that the data protected by this primitive is likely tainted
& (some invariant is not being upheld).
M The specifics of how this "poisoned" state affects other threads and whether
K the panics are recognized reliably or on a best-effort basis depend on the
, primitive. See [Overview](#overview) below.
_ The synchronization objects in this module have alternative implementations that do not employ
2 poisoning in the [`std::sync::nonpoison`] module.
1 [`std::sync::nonpoison`]: crate::sync::nonpoison

= with a high-level overview for each object and a description
 of how it employs "poisoning".
4   Condition variables are typically associated with
1   a boolean predicate (a condition) and a mutex.
C   This implementation is associated with [`poison::Mutex`](Mutex),
   which employs poisoning.
E   For this reason, [`Condvar::wait()`] will return a [`LockResult`],
9   just like [`poison::Mutex::lock()`](Mutex::lock) does.
J   Panicking while holding the lock typically poisons the mutex, but it is
@   not guaranteed to detect this condition in all circumstances.
K   [`Mutex::lock()`] returns a [`LockResult`], providing a way to deal with
O   the poisoned state. See [`Mutex`'s documentation](Mutex#poisoning) for more.
L   This implementation, like [`Mutex`], usually becomes poisoned on a panic.
I   Note, however, that an `RwLock` may only be poisoned if a panic occurs
P   while it is locked exclusively (write mode). If a panic occurs in any reader,
&   then the lock will not be poisoned.

? A mutual exclusion primitive useful for protecting shared data
L This mutex will block threads waiting for the lock to become available. The
P mutex can be created via a [`new`] constructor. Each mutex has a type parameter
O which represents the data that it is protecting. The data can only be accessed
G through the RAII guards returned from [`lock`] and [`try_lock`], which
I guarantees that the data is only ever accessed when the mutex is locked.
K The mutexes in this module implement a strategy called "poisoning" where a
G mutex becomes poisoned if it recognizes that the thread holding it has
 panicked.
M Once a mutex is poisoned, all other threads are unable to access the data by
L default as it is likely tainted (some invariant is not being upheld). For a
F mutex, this means that the [`lock`] and [`try_lock`] methods return a
J [`Result`] which indicates whether a mutex has been poisoned or not. Most
L usage of a mutex will simply [`unwrap()`] these results, propagating panics
L among threads to ensure that a possibly invalid invariant is not witnessed.
K Poisoning is only advisory: the [`PoisonError`] type has an [`into_inner`]
K method which will return the guard that would have otherwise been returned
M on a successful lock. This allows access to the data, despite the lock being
 poisoned.
J In addition, the panic detection is not ideal, so even unpoisoned mutexes
J need to be handled with care, since certain panics may have been skipped.
D Here is a non-exhaustive list of situations where this might occur:
I - If a mutex is locked while a panic is underway, e.g. within a [`Drop`]
L   implementation or a [panic hook], panicking for the second time while the
M   lock is held will leave the mutex unpoisoned. Note that while double panic
A   usually aborts the program, [`catch_unwind`] can prevent this.
K - Locking and unlocking the mutex across different panic contexts, e.g. by
I   storing the guard to a [`Cell`] within [`Drop::drop`] and accessing it
L   outside, or vice versa, can affect poisoning status in an unexpected way.
K - Foreign exceptions do not currently trigger poisoning even in absence of
   other panics.
J While this rarely happens in realistic code, `unsafe` code cannot rely on
G poisoning for soundness, since the behavior of poisoning can depend on
F outside context. Here's an example of **incorrect** use of poisoning:
 struct MutexBox<T> {
     data: Mutex<*mut T>,

2         // hook, this can lead to use-after-free.

 [`forget()`]: mem::forget
 A reader-writer lock
J This type of lock allows a number of readers or at most one writer at any
L point in time. The write portion of this lock typically allows modification
L of the underlying data (exclusive access) and the read portion of this lock
7 typically allows for read-only access (shared access).
K In comparison, a [`Mutex`] does not distinguish between readers or writers
N that acquire the lock, therefore blocking any threads waiting for the lock to
I become available. An `RwLock` will allow multiple readers to acquire the
2 lock as long as a writer is not holding the lock.
I The priority policy of the lock is dependent on the underlying operating
C system's implementation, and this type does not guarantee that any
L particular policy will be used. In particular, a writer which is waiting to
I acquire the lock in `write` might or might not block concurrent calls to
 `read`, e.g.:
7 <details><summary>Potential deadlock example</summary>
( // Thread 1              |  // Thread 2

*                          |  // will block
4                          |  let _wg = lock.write();
 // may deadlock          |
 let _rg2 = lock.read();  |
J The type parameter `T` represents the data that this lock protects. It is
E required that `T` satisfies [`Send`] to be shared across threads and
E [`Sync`] to allow concurrent access through readers. The RAII guards
H returned from the locking methods implement [`Deref`] (and [`DerefMut`]
E for the `write` methods) to allow access to the content of the lock.
N An `RwLock`, like [`Mutex`], will [usually] become poisoned on a panic. Note,
M however, that an `RwLock` may only be poisoned if a panic occurs while it is
K locked exclusively (write mode). If a panic occurs in any reader, then the
 lock will not be poisoned.
" [usually]: super::Mutex#poisoning

A should only appear in binaries built assuming OS support: `std`.

G [`f32`] and [`f64`] achieve a similar trick by using "lang items", but

Portable SIMD module.
This module offers a portable abstraction for SIMD operations
that is not bound to any particular hardware architecture.
# What is "portable"?
This module provides a SIMD implementation that is fast and predictable on any target.
### Portable SIMD works on every target
Unlike target-specific SIMD in `std::arch`, portable SIMD compiles for every target.
In this regard, it is just like "regular" Rust.
### Portable SIMD is consistent between targets
A program using portable SIMD can expect identical behavior on any target.
In most regards, [`Simd<T, N>`] can be thought of as a parallelized `[T; N]` and operates like a sequence of `T`.
This has one notable exception: a handful of older architectures (e.g. `armv7` and `powerpc`) flush [subnormal](`f32::is_subnormal`) `f32` values to zero.
On these architectures, subnormal `f32` input values are replaced with zeros, and any operation producing subnormal `f32` values produces zeros instead.
This doesn't affect most architectures or programs.
### Operations use the best instructions available
Operations provided by this module compile to the best available SIMD instructions.
Portable SIMD is not a low-level vendor library, and operations in portable SIMD _do not_ necessarily map to a single instruction.
Instead, they map to a reasonable implementation of the operation for the target.
Consistency between targets is not compromised to use faster or fewer instructions.
In some cases, `std::arch` will provide a faster function that has slightly different behavior than the `std::simd` equivalent.
For example, `_mm_min_ps`[^1] can be slightly faster than [`SimdFloat::simd_min`](`num::SimdFloat::simd_min`), but does not conform to the IEEE standard also used by [`f32::min`].
When necessary, [`Simd<T, N>`] can be converted to the types provided by `std::arch` to make use of target-specific functions.
Many targets simply don't have SIMD, or don't support SIMD for a particular element type.
In those cases, regular scalar operations are generated instead.
[^1]: `_mm_min_ps(x, y)` is equivalent to `x.simd_lt(y).select(x, y)`

#This module provides support for automatic differentiation. For precise information on
differences between the `autodiff_forward` and `autodiff_reverse` macros and how to
use them, see their respective documentation.
## General usage
Autodiff macros can be applied to almost all function definitions, see below for examples.
They can be applied to functions accepting structs, arrays, slices, vectors, tuples, and more.
It is possible to apply multiple autodiff macros to the same function. As an example, this can
be helpful to compute the partial derivatives with respect to `x` and `y` independently:
```rust,ignore (optional component)
#[autodiff_forward(dsquare1, Dual, Const, Dual)]
#[autodiff_forward(dsquare2, Const, Dual, Dual)]
#[autodiff_forward(dsquare3, Active, Active, Active)]
fn square(x: f64, y: f64) -> f64 {
  x * x + 2.0 * y
We also support autodiff on functions with generic parameters:
```rust,ignore (optional component)
#[autodiff_forward(generic_derivative, Duplicated, Active)]
fn generic_f<T: std::ops::Mul<Output = T> + Copy>(x: &T) -> T {
 x * x
or applying autodiff to nested functions:
```rust,ignore (optional component)
fn outer(x: f64) -> f64 {
  #[autodiff_forward(inner_derivative, Dual, Const)]
  fn inner(y: f64) -> f64 {
    y * y
  inner_derivative(x, 1.0)
fn main() {
    assert_eq!(outer(3.14), 6.28);
The generated function will be available in the same scope as the function differentiated, and
have the same private/pub usability.
## Traits and impls
Autodiff macros can be used in multiple ways in combination with traits:
```rust,ignore (optional component)
struct Foo {
    a: f64,
trait MyTrait {
    #[autodiff_reverse(df, Const, Active, Active)]
    fn f(&self, x: f64) -> f64;
impl MyTrait for Foo {
    fn f(&self, x: f64) -> f64 {
        x.sin()
    }
fn main() {
    let foo = Foo { a: 3.0f64 };
    assert_eq!(foo.f(2.0), 2.0_f64.sin());
    assert_eq!(foo.df(2.0, 1.0).1, 2.0_f64.cos());
In this case `df` will be the default implementation provided by the library who provided the
trait. A user implementing `MyTrait` could then decide to use the default implementation of
`df`, or overwrite it with a custom implementation as a form of "custom derivatives".
On the other hand, a function generated by either autodiff macro can also be used to implement a
trait:
```rust,ignore (optional component)
struct Foo {
    a: f64,
trait MyTrait {
    fn f(&self, x: f64) -> f64;
    fn df(&self, x: f64, seed: f64) -> (f64, f64);
impl MyTrait for Foo {
    #[autodiff_reverse(df, Const, Active, Active)]
    fn f(&self, x: f64) -> f64 {
        self.a * 0.25 * (x * x - 1.0 - 2.0 * x.ln())
    }
Simple `impl` blocks without traits are also supported. Differentiating with respect to the
implemented struct will then require the use of a "shadow struct" to hold the derivatives of the
struct fields:
```rust,ignore (optional component)
struct OptProblem {
    a: f64,
    b: f64,
impl OptProblem {
    #[autodiff_reverse(d_objective, Duplicated, Duplicated, Duplicated)]
    fn objective(&self, x: &[f64], out: &mut f64) {
        *out = self.a + x[0].sqrt() * self.b
    }
fn main() {
    let p = OptProblem { a: 1., b: 2. };
    let mut p_shadow = OptProblem { a: 0., b: 0. };
    let mut dx = [0.0];
    let mut out = 0.0;
    let mut dout = 1.0;
    p.d_objective(&mut p_shadow, &x, &mut dx, &mut out, &mut dout);
## Higher-order derivatives
Finally, it is possible to generate higher-order derivatives (e.g. Hessian) by applying an
autodiff macro to a function that is already generated by an autodiff macro, via a thin wrapper.
The following example uses Forward mode over Reverse mode
```rust,ignore (optional component)
#[autodiff_reverse(df, Duplicated, Duplicated)]
fn f(x: &[f64;2], y: &mut f64) {
  *y = x[0] * x[0] + x[1] * x[0]
#[autodiff_forward(h, Dual, Dual, Dual, Dual)]
fn wrapper(x: &[f64;2], dx: &mut [f64;2], y: &mut f64, dy: &mut f64) {
  df(x, dx, y, dy);
fn main() {
    let mut y = 0.0;
    let x = [2.0, 2.0];
    let mut dy = 0.0;
    let mut dx = [1.0, 0.0];
    let mut bx = [0.0, 0.0];
    let mut by = 1.0;
    let mut dbx = [0.0, 0.0];
    let mut dby = 0.0;
    h(&x, &mut dx, &mut bx, &mut dbx, &mut y, &mut dy, &mut by, &mut dby);
    assert_eq!(&dbx, [2.0, 1.0]);

- Builds without `lto="fat"` are not yet supported.
- Builds in debug mode are currently more likely to fail compilation.
